## Global Outlook Calm on the surface

We prefer global equities to fixed income once more, given a backdrop of solid global growth and a gradual easing cycle. But investors should keep a wary eye on tail scenarios, especially in light of political risks.

## US Immigration

A mighty tailwind. . . 21
Surging US immigration has helped boost economic growth, reduce inflation, and fuel
expectations of monetary easing. With policy measures threatening to upend the status quo, we
quantify immigration flows and assess likely efects on the outlook under alternative scenarios.

## Global Trade

Back to the bad old days?. . . . 45
China's manufacturing overcapacity is colliding with eforts by the West to de-risk supply chains
and protect strategic industries. The impact of recent tarifs is still limited, but risk of an
escalation is rising. Some ‘connector’ countries may benefit from tarifs on China, but the global
economy in aggregate would lose.

## FX Strategy

Currency wars, China-style. . . 64
China's stealth currency debasement has supported trade competitiveness, but provoked a
backlash from the G3 while fuelling capital outflows. JPY and KRW weakness is adding more
pressure on the CNY. As the USD rallies broadly, the CNY and much of Asia will need to follow
suit, with potential tarifs posing additional risks.

Foreword

## Calm on the surface

The global macro backdrop is one of steady growth, sticky inflation and slow easing by central banks. Equities should outperform core fixed income yet again. But with political risks rising, and given relatively low volatility in equity and currency markets , it is also a good time to pick up downside protection.

FOCUS

Ajay Rajadhyaksha<sup>(i)</sup>
+1 212 412 7669
ajay.rajadhyaksha@barclays.com
BCI, US

##  Listen

It has been another good quarter for investors. Global equities have made a new high, led by the miracle that is US Big Tech. Credit spreads are near their tights, bond yields have been wellbehaved, and so has oil. Perhaps the biggest risk factor has been the USD’s sustained strength against large Asian exporter currencies like the Japanese yen. All in all, the second quarter of 2024 has been largely drama-free. If you had exposure to growth assets at the start of April, you went home happy.

Underpinning these benign markets is steady global growth, with major players balancing each other. China is having a weak Q2, after a strong first quarter. We see near-flat growth in Q2 in China after a 12.5% (q/q saar) surge in Q1. The second half should also be disappointing, with growth below 4% (q/q saar). On the other hand, the Euro area is finally seeing a cyclical recovery after two years of stagnation. The first quarter saw 1.3% (q/q saar) growth – decent by Euro area standards – and the second half of the year should be similar. India has been an outperformer for a couple of years and looks set to grow at around 7% in 2024 as well. But one of the biggest reasons for a solid macro backdrop is still the world’s largest economy.

Every so often this cycle, the "US is finally running out of steam" narrative seems to resurface. It happened in the summer of 2022, then from March to May 2023, followed by November/ December of last year. Most recently, a few slightly softer data points in April led to renewed interest in the "US is finally turning down" theme. Yet the underlying economy has remained very stable. For example, the 3mma of payroll growth is almost 250k now, similar to the trend over the past year. Domestic final sales grew 2.8% in Q1, very close to the 2023 average. Sure, there is some anecdotal evidence that lower-end consumers are feeling a pinch. And small businesses have been gloomy for several months. But there has also been an massive expansion in household wealth since the start of COVID, in both equities and home prices. Moreover, US tech continues to spend aggressively on its machine learning build-out. We expect the US to expand 2.5% in 2024, right in line with 2023.

This is a Special Report that is not an equity or a debt research report under U.S. FINRA Rules 2241-2242.

(i) This author is a debt research analyst in the Fixed Income, Currencies and Commodities Research department and is neither an equity research analyst nor subject to all of the independence and disclosure standards applicable to analysts who produce debt research reports under U.S. FINRA Rule 2242.

While there have been few surprises on growth this year, the same can’t be said of price data. Last week’s inflation data in the US was admittedly a pleasant surprise, but we think a number of one-ofs that contributed to the soft print will reverse in June. Core PCE inflation y/y should finish 2024 at 2.8%, not far of where we ended 2023. Europe had better inflation data in Q1 than the US, but services inflation has been sticky recently, and played a role in removing any lingering chances of a July cut. The UK April CPI print blew past expectations and pushed out an expected June cut by the BoE. Across the West, the ‘last mile’ on inflation is proving to be slow and bumpy.

In general, the combination of steady growth and sticky inflation has led markets to price in fewer rate cuts, and we agree. In the US, we expect the Fed to cut just once in 2024. In Europe, Lagarde has all but confirmed that the ECB will sit out the summer; September is the next "live" meeting. The BoE is likely to start lowering rates in August, well after national elections are over. It is hard to characterize any of these central bank actions as easing "cycles." Historically, rate cut cycles play out quickly and against a backdrop of high job losses and quickly slowing activity; that is not the case in any major Western economy right now. Instead, while they wait for data that confirms inflation is decisively headed towards 2%, central banks are simply turning a little less restrictive – at a very slow pace. Put bluntly, as summer starts, the macro backdrop is not very worrying — but neither does it seem terribly exciting.

There are plenty of concerning developments below the surface. Wars in Europe and the Middle East, persistent worries about the US fiscal situation, concerns from the rest of the world (especially JPY investors!) that the Fed is keeping rates too high for too long – all of these are legitimate worries. Already, election results in Mexico, India and South Africa have caused upheavals in local markets. The UK elections will likely (if polls are correct) bring in a new government, while the French parliamentary elections could bring the National Rally to power, raising questions about European co-operation. Trade frictions are rising, with the US and EU both imposing new tarifs on China. As we approach Q4, attention should turn to the US elections as polls solidify and the prospect of a global trade war looms. Our baseline is that none of these developments – or others we might be missing – will knock the global economy of course. It does concern us that, with equities making new highs virtually every week, investors seem blasé about downside risks.

Nowhere is this more apparent than in the option markets, with implied volatility relatively low in most major asset classes (rates being the notable exception). This leaves us in an uncomfortable position when it comes to asset allocation. We see little upside in core fixed income, given our growth and inflation outlook and rising fiscal deficits in many economies. Nor do we find equities compelling, after the massive rally from last October’s lows, especially with broad indices unusually hostage to a few megacap US tech firms (most notably NVDA). But we feel that the path of least resistance is still for equities to grind out positive returns. So we find ourselves overweight stocks over bonds for yet one more quarter. This time around, though, we recommend that investors use low levels of vol in equities and FX to buy tail risks, as we discuss in our Investment Outlook.

Don’t be taken in by the summer lull. The second half of the year will bring both risks and opportunities. It is our hope that this publication helps you, our readers, navigate financial markets successfully in the months ahead. Happy reading.

Agy Payd

Macro Outlook

# Enjoy the quiet (while it lasts)

Our baseline is for steady global growth, uneven but continued progress on inflation, and a slow easing cycle, despite rising political risks. Disappointing China data should be ofset by resilient growth in the US and India, as well as a cyclical pickup in Europe.

• Markets have been bufeted by rising political risks in France, Mexico and India, while monetary policy uncertainty persists in the US, Europe, and Japan. Even so, we expect the world economy to stay resilient and grow 3.1%, just below last year’s 3.2%.

• Investors are increasingly questioning the strength of the economic expansion in the US. We feel these concerns are overdone and are driven by weakness in consumer and business surveys, which have had a poor correlation to data in this cycle. The US labor market remains strong, household wealth has gone up enormously, and Big Tech spending on AI continues to rise.

• That said, we expect weakness in China. Manufacturing, services, and construction are all in contraction, and the property sector shows few signs of bottoming. On the other hand, Europe is having a cyclical rebound, after several quarters of stagnation. PMIs suggest that the strength has continued into Q2, with improvement across geographies and industries.

• The 'last mile' on inflation has been a bumpy process across the West. Services inflation has been sticky on both sides of the Atlantic. While US inflation in May was a welcome relief, some of the decline is unlikely to persist.  We expect Q4/Q4 core PCE to be 2.8%, still far from 2%. The uneven progress on inflation means central bank easing is likely to be very slow this year, with the Fed cutting just once.

• The pace of JPY weakness has picked up this year, and China may face competitive devaluation pressure if the USD goes on another run. The risks of a global trade war are rising, given China's massive goods surplus and the prospect of heavy US tarifs after the elections.

• Overall, we do not expect these risks to knock the world economy of course. But with risky assets at all-time highs, investors should keep a wary eye on tail scenarios.

As we were finalizing this quarterly publication last week, markets were hit with a series of notable developments. Two weekends ago, President Macron surprised and spooked financial markets by announcing snap parliamentary elections in France. French government debt sold of, amidst speculation that the President might resign if the far right won the elections.

Last Wednesday brought an unexpectedly weak US CPI report, followed by a surprisingly hawkish Fed dot-plot, in turn followed by a very weak PPI print. The BoJ meeting showed how JPY depreciation is now starting to become a real problem for monetary policy. Last but not least, the Mexican peso continued to spiral on the heels of President-elect Sheinbaum’s stronger-than-expected victory, including a fierce unwind of the MXNJPY carry trade.

And yet, we found ourselves not having to change a single major central bank forecast since the start of last week – a tribute to how steady the global economy has been this year. Amidst all the sound and fury of recent weeks, the world is still growing at the same pace as 2023, with inflation that is sticky but considerably lower than a year ago. Valuations might seem full, but this is still a supportive environment for risk assets. The question at hand is: how long can  it continue?

## Reports of the death of the US consumer have been greatly exaggerated

In recent weeks, most client questions have centered on the prospect of an economic slowdown, rather than the opposite risk that economies overheat. After all, the US has seen softer data since April and is the only major economy at risk of overheating; no one really expects either Europe or China to push up global inflation.

The ‘US is finally turning down’ hypothesis rests on two pillars: gloomy surveys and fading fiscal expansion. On the household side, the University of Michigan’s current conditions index has turned down for several months and is far below pre-COVID levels. Conference Board surveys have trended sideways recently, but once again are much below where they were in late 2019. Clients who are economic bears seem most focused on the NFIB survey of small businesses, where the headline confidence index as well as hiring intentions have dropped sharply. The argument is that corporate margins are too wide, and as they go down from current levels, firms will respond by laying of workers.

Fiscal expansion supported the US through much of the hiking cycle. Excess household savings allowed consumers to spend despite several quarters of negative real wages, and manufacturing construction rose very sharply from H2 22 (helped by the CHIPS Act, the IIJA and the IRA). But structures-investment growth has flattened out recently, if at a high level. And while the amount of excess household savings left is a moving target (our estimate is \$850bn chained \$2017), everyone will agree that the quantum is far smaller than a couple of years ago. It seems just a matter of time, the bears tell us, before household-savings rates have to rise from currently low levels and consumption pulls back.

Not so fast. The relationship between surveys and actual consumption has been very weak, to the point of being non-existent this entire cycle. And while small businesses have been unhappy for a while, we don’t see anything in their hiring/separations data that suggests they are behaving diferently from large corporates. Consider the actual state of the labor market. The 3-, 6- and 12-month average of jobs created are remarkably similar, all near 250k. Both initial and continuing claims are still low, and have been for many quarters. At the margin, the labor market is coming into more balance – as seen in various aspects of the JOLTS survey (quits, vacancies, etc.). But the payroll proxy (hours worked times wages times jobs) is running at 6% annualized over 3 months, not far of the 5.5% over the last year, which means income growth is still strong.

And then there is the wealth efect. US household wealth adjusted for inflation is up a staggering \$19.1trn between Q4 2019 and Q1 2024. In nominal terms, the amount is around \$40trn; it is as if each 4-person household in the US was given roughly half a million dollars since COVID. Critics will complain that wealth efects are unequally distributed. Sure, but they are always unequally distributed and yet, they normally support consumption. In fact, because home prices have risen so strongly since COVID, wealth efects are likely more evenly spread out this cycle than in the past. With such a large tailwind behind them, it is hard for us to see consumers pull back sharply in the absence of a large exogenous shock. Consumer spending, net hiring, and payroll income are locked in a resilient and self-reinforcing virtuous cycle, supercharged by gains in household wealth. Add in a continued surge in Big Tech spending (US tech firms spent over \$200bn last year on their AI build-out, with this number set to rise sharply in 2024), and we have the US growing at 2.5% this year, exactly in line with 2023.

## China – a strong start but a weak finish

The world’s second-largest economy, however, is set to slow down. Oficial GDP growth in Q1 surprised greatly to the upside; q/q growth (saar) rose to a near four-year high of 12.7%. But this big surprise largely reflected strong data in the first two months, led by industrial production and exports. From March onwards, activity, trade, credit growth and price data all started coming in weaker. Retail sales in March disappointed, IP growth surprised to the downside, and the contraction in property investment deepened. April was more of the same, with retail sales once again missing expectations (for the sixth straight month). A deeper contraction in auto sales amidst fierce price wars was especially worrying, since price cuts could help entrench deflationary expectations among households.

The jobs picture is another concern. The employment indices for manufacturing, services, and construction all remained deep in contraction for the fourth consecutive month in May. In particular, the employment index for the construction sector fell to a 2-year low of 43.3, while services PMI employment hit a 4-month low of 46.7. MoF data shows that revenues from individual income tax contracted 7% y/y YTD in April, worse than Q1’s 4.5% contraction. Oficial unemployment rates remain stable for now, but we are seeing more negative news of pay cuts and layofs in both traditional (property) and new (EVs and solar panels) sectors. May data also brought other signs of softness; new export orders fell back into contraction and there are signs that industrial profits are deteriorating.

And finally, there is the all-important property sector, where we see no signs of a bottom. Yes, the authorities have avoided a worst-case scenario, where a series of developers go bankrupt and cannot finish pre-sold projects. While many developers have defaulted on ofshore dollar debt, the local banking sector has rolled loans over and prioritized debt restructuring, which has prevented chaotic defaults (and social unrest). But measures like cutting mortgage rates and reducing down payments have largely failed to support demand. For example, new starts (-23% y/y) and property sales (-21% y/y) continued to contract in May, and new sales are down 60-70% from the 2021 peak. All 70 cities we track continue to show month-on-month declines in home prices. The China Index Academy survey in April noted that fears of continued falls in home prices are one of the top reasons for a lack of demand, coupled with unstable incomes and the pressure of existing loans.

Finally, there is no Big Bang stimulus coming. The RMB 300-500bn that the PBoC recently announced for clearing inventory is tiny compared to the Chinese real-estate sector. We expect a sharp slowdown in growth to only 0.2% q/q saar in Q2 (partly as payback for Q1), and 3.5% growth in the second half of this year. And if export strength cannot be sustained, the economy could face a 'triple whammy' from property, consumption and exports. China might have started 2024 strong, but the rest of the year is likely to be weak.

Asia’s other demographic giant, India, has seen a political upheaval – along the lines of what we saw in Mexico and at roughly the same time. The Modi government was expected to win a sweeping majority but has now been forced to form a coalition with smaller parties, after underperforming at the polls. But Prime Minister Modi has now been sworn in for a third fiveyear term, and investor confidence has recovered far more quickly than in Mexico. While a

coalition might make far-reaching reforms a little more dificult, this is still an economy that should grow 7.2% in 2024.

## A cyclical rebound in Europe, but French political risks are rising

The final GDP print for the first quarter showed the euro area breaking out of five quarters of stagnation, growing 0.3% q/q. Final sales to domestic purchasers excluding IPP (our preferred measure of growth) grew by 0.27% q/q. Services led the way (up 0.5% q/q), but industrial output fell for the fifth straight quarter (-0.4% q/q), and is well below its Feb 2022 level. Investment was very weak, but was ofset by strong net exports; trade added 0.9% q/q to growth. The Q1 GDP deflator decomposition shows that profit margins are normalizing and absorbing higher unit labor costs; firms have accumulated substantial margin bufers over the past two years. While compensation per employee did rise to 5.1% y/y in Q1, from 4.6% in Q4 23, we believe that this reflects a catch-up from prior inflation; there is little risk of a wage spiral.

PMIs and EC surveys both point to the recovery extending into Q2. The euro area’s most recent Economic Sentiment Indicator, for example, shows broad-based improvement in geographies and industries. PMIs and the IFO survey both suggest that German manufacturing orders are going to accelerate. Households are also turning more optimistic, especially in Germany and France, and the euro area jobless rate is at a record low of 6.4%. Admittedly, the credit cycle isn’t turning up; net credit flows have been flat for two years. But after 15 months of stagnation, Europe looks likely to enjoy a mild cyclical recovery in 2024-25.

But just as data have started to improve, political risk has come back. It started with the EU Parliamentary elections, where right-wing parties in Germany (AFD) and France (National Rally) made gains. But political risk really ramped up when President Macron responded with snap parliamentary elections. Amid speculation that the President was considering resigning (speculation that Macron dismissed), European equities pulled back, OAT-bund spreads widened and the EUR weakened.

Most polls point to a hung Parliament, with the National Rally as the largest party but without a majority. A hung Parliament with President Macron's coalition losing more seats would make governing even more dificult than it has been since he lost his majority in 2022. The government would struggle to pass any meaningful legislation to improve public finances or promote growth; gridlock and repeated no confidence votes are likely. We think the government might be able to pass a seven-year adjustment plan that the EC accepts, but little else.

There is also a chance that National Rally gets close enough that it can form a government with a few very small parties. Such a ‘cohabitation’ between President Macron and National Rally would, in our view, be received more negatively than a hung Parliament. The president would likely end up a lame duck on domestic issues including the economy, and any prospects for European integration would be ruled out. National Rally leader Marine Le Pen in the past has been associated with ideas such as France leaving the EU and the eurozone, as well as NATO. At the very least, markets will worry that a far-right government will undermine European institutions and worsen France’s long-term fiscal sustainability.

Finally, investors cannot ignore the tail risk that if President Macron’s party gets a severe drubbing, he decides to resign (even if the President has ruled that out for now) and National Rally ends up controlling both Parliament and the Elysee. Political risk is likely to stay elevated through higher risk premium in European financial assets until at least July 7, and possibly for months beyond.

## The 'last mile’ on inflation is proving to be bumpy

Last week saw a major US bond rally, sparked by a surprisingly weak CPI and PPI report. Core CPI came in at just 0.16% m/m, the lowest monthly print since August 2021. Core services exshelter (a metric favored by Chair Powell) fell to -0.05% from 0.46%. The disinflationary bias was broad-based. No major category surprised to the upside and the Cleveland Fed’s ‘trimmed mean’ had its smallest month-to-month rise in more than three years. It was an excellent inflation print, no question, and should ease the Fed worries after Q1’s inflation data. We now expect May core PCE to be just 0.08% m/m, a welcome drop from 0.25% in April and the 4% annualized run rate over the first 4 months of the year.

But this report somewhat flatters to deceive. Of the 13bp drop in core CPI (from the prior month), 11bp came from transportation services. This magnitude of slowing is unsustainable and there should some payback next month. For example, the price decline in auto insurance was the first since October 2021 and came against the trend of recent months.

Individual inflation prints are tricky to read – in some months, everything that can go wrong does, with all the ‘one-ofs’ surprising in one direction. January 2024 was an example, with core PCE up a shocking 0.48% m/m. And in some CPI reports, everything seems to go right, as was the case last week. We think core PCE is back to 0.2% m/m (rounded) in June, and note that the second half of the year will bring dificult comparisons for the y/y number, given the run of low inflation prints in H2 23. We expect y/y core PCE to be at 2.8% at the end of 2024, still far away from 2%.

Meanwhile, services prices have been stubbornly sticky in Europe recently. Euro-area services inflation jumped 0.4% in May to 4.1%, pushing both headline and core considerably above consensus. And in the UK, April services CPI (released in late May) came in at a problematic 5.9% y/y versus the BoE forecast of 5.5%. Markets responded quickly; moving a June cut from a morethan 50% probability to virtually none within a matter of hours. A similar theme is echoing in Japan, which finally seems to be sustainably emerging from decades of deflation; services PPI reached a three-decade high recently, even if ‘BoJ core’ CPI is still just below 2%. All said, the West’s substantial progress on inflation in 2023 is not reversing this year. But the last mile on inflation – the point at which we are sustainably at 2% - is likely to be slow and bumpy.

## Easing – slowly – into rate cuts

The ECB kicked of rate cuts in its June meeting, but we are reluctant to call it the start of easing cycles in the West. Central banks usually take the escalator up during hikes; interest rate increases are slow and measured. And they take the elevator down when easing policy; rate cut cycles usually occur quickly, amidst financial and economic stress, and often with a 50bp cut as the first move. The last three years have been unusual. Central banks took the figurative elevator up during hikes; the Fed famously surprised markets with a 75bp hike in June 2022 and then followed it up with two more 75bp moves. And even as the ECB has finally started easing, President Lagarde made it clear that June would not be followed by a July cut. Across the English channel, the BoE was forced to push back its expected June cut due to stronger inflation data a couple of weeks ago.

And in the US, the Fed largely played down last week’s surprisingly small price rises. The Fed’s 2024 dot-plot moved from three cuts to just one for 2024, in line with our view but against market consensus of two cuts. Chair Powell struck a cautious tone. While he acknowledged a gradual cooling in the labor market and the soft May CPI, he also emphasized that the FOMC was not confident yet that inflation was sustainably on a track to 2%. The median projection for GDP growth remained unchanged at 2.1% (q4/q4) for 2024, despite Q1 GDP coming in at 1.3%, suggesting that the FOMC believes growth will be reasonably strong for the rest of the year. The FOMC also continued to move its longer-term dot up a little more; it is now at 2.75%. May inflation might have been welcome news, but the FOMC clearly feels it is far too soon to sound the all-clear on inflation, and we tend to agree.

Our forecast is for the Fed to start the easing cycle in September, despite the optics of being seen to start easing in the last meeting before November elections. But we could just as easily be convinced that the FOMC will wait until December; the US economy is solid enough and inflation sticky enough that a few slightly stronger-than-expected data points could convince the Fed to wait. We also expect the ECB to sit out July and likely cut again in its September meeting. The BoE, we feel, will finally start easing in August, having been thwarted in June by data. Meanwhile, the BoJ also took a cautious approach in last week’s MPM, dashing expectations of investors for a detailed schedule for QT. We think the Japanese central bank will hike in July, but it will be the only time this year. Central banks across the West are likely to be incremental in their actions for the next few months.

In sum, this is a world where growth is still solid, if far from spectacular. The US remains the engine, Indian growth is providing a strong boost, and Chinese weakness is being ofset to some extent by a cyclical European rebound. We expect the world economy to grow at 3.1% this year, just below last year’s 3.2%.

## US elections approach, as does the risk of trade wars

A couple of downside risks now loom in the not-too-distant future. Much of the West is now complaining about Chinese manufacturing overcapacity. China’s merchandise trade surplus (as a share of world GDP) is much larger than the surpluses that Germany and Japan ran at their respective peaks. While China’s remarkable progress in electric vehicles has drawn headlines, the country is also dominant in clean technology manufacturing, and focused on exporting electronics, higher value-added white goods, etc.

As China expert Michael Pettis noted a few months ago: "China accounts for 18% of global GDP,…only 13% of global consumption and an astonishing 32% of global investment."<sup>1</sup>  And the country seems focused on upgrading its advanced manufacturing capabilities as the driver of growth. A number of China experts from academia and policy have repeatedly told us that China doesn’t want to mimic the American economic model; rather, it wants to become the next Germany.

The scale of China's goods surplus is provoking reactions, and not just in the US. Last week, the European Commission concluded that China’s electric-vehicle value chain benefits from unfair subsidization, hurting European firms. The EU announced 17-38% tarifs on Chinese EV producers, in efect from July 4. This afects about EUR10bn of Chinese car exports, and presumably China will respond. On the US side, President Biden imposed two rounds of tarifs on China last month, on roughly \$18bn of imports in strategically important sectors (including a 100% tax on Chinese electric vehicles).

When it comes to tarifs though, the biggest focus is on former President Trump’s campaign, which has repeatedly promised heavy tarifs. This includes a blanket 10% tarif on all imports into the US, as well as much larger tarifs on specific imports from exporting countries, including China but also Europe and others. And last week, the former President mooted the idea of scrapping income tax altogether and ofsetting the revenue loss with an ‘all-tarif’ policy.

The 2018 trade war between the US and China seemed to avoid the worst-case outcomes critics had feared. While the US imposed tarifs on around \$250bn of Chinese imports, and China reacted with tarifs on some US exports, research by the Stanford center showed that Chinese exports to the US dropped by just under 5% and overall exports dropped by less than 1%. At a macro level, the global economy expanded in 2018 at the exact same pace as 2017 (3.9%). The US accelerated to 2.9% growth, while China slowed down from 6.9% in 2017 to 6.6%. Of course, other factors (US tax cuts, China stimulus, Fed hikes, etc.) played a major role beyond just the trade war. But the tail downside scenarios critics had feared never came to pass.

However, the risks associated with a global trade war are greater this time, for several reasons. Domestic demand is weaker in China now than in 2018; exporting its way to growth is a core aim for the country now. There is unusually bipartisan support in the US for tarifs on China; the Biden government never withdrew tarifs imposed by the Trump Administration. China’s goodstrade surplus has never been larger. The US is in an enviable macro position relative to both China and Europe – an energy superpower, a technology superpower, a massive recipient of capital, the rise of US Big Tech (a few firms are more valuable than most of the world’s equity markets), etc. The country has a national industrial policy (the IIJA, the IRA, the CHIPS Act) for the first time in decades. Most important, the Trump campaign is no longer talking about targeted tarifs, but blanket tarifs on all exports, followed by much larger tarifs on Chinese (and even European) imports than in 2018.

In 'Back to the bad old days?', we discuss the prospects of a new global trade war – what could start it, and what the repercussions could be. This is one of the biggest risks for the world economy entering 2025 and will be in increasing focus as fall approaches.

Another macro question related to US politics is what happens if US labor supply is constrained by a slowing in the flow of migrant workers. President Biden’s recent executive order clamps down on migration by closing ports if certain numeric thresholds are breached. And former President Trump has not only vowed to crack down on unauthorized immigration, but also said his government will round up and deport millions of undocumented workers. In 'A mighty tailwind', we discuss the macro impact of immigration on the economy, including possible scenarios in 2025.

And finally on the elections itself, polls suggest the Senate is likely to go Republican; the Democrats are defending far more seats and Senator Joe Manchin is not running for re-election in West Virginia. The House is more likely than not to go Democratic. And the Presidential election remains too close to call in our view, dependent on factors like early voting, electionday turnout, etc. in just a handful of states. US politics is increasingly likely to drive market focus as November approaches.

## If the US is too strong, that is a problem too – for others

A more immediate risk – well before November – might come from Asian currency markets. The BoJ is running the most accommodative central policy amongst Western central banks, and the USDJPY cross has been rising for many months. The pace of JPY weakness has picked up this year, after three strong US inflation prints led to rate cuts being priced out of the US curve. Japanese oficials have repeatedly warned that they will punish speculators weakening the yen. In late April, USDJPY touched a multi-decade high of over 158. The Japanese Ministry of Finance (MoF) intervened in currency markets that weekend and a number of times since. The MoF has now spent at least \$62bn defending the JPY, more than what it spent in 2022. The US has seen slightly softer data over the past six weeks, 10y Treasuries have rallied from 4.7% to near 4.2% and USDJPY is still around 157.5, close to levels before intervention started.

This begs the question – what happens if the US sees stronger data and markets start pushing back rate cuts further? The interest-rate gap between the US and the Japanese rate curve is considerable; over 500bp at the front end and more than 325bp at the 10y point despite the recent US bond rally. It is not a gap that the BoJ can reasonably close any time soon; no one seriously expects the policy rate in Japan to rise to say 1.5% or 2% quickly. That means – if markets believe that the Fed will have to stay on hold for much longer – the JPY could easily weaken further, including past the psychological 160 level.

A new run of USD strength against the JPY would also create more problems for the PBoC. Japan is a major exporter just like China, China is leaning more heavily on exports for growth, and CNYJPY is now at its strongest levels ever. For the last few months, the CNY has appreciated against its trade-weighted basket as the Chinese authorities held on to their quasi-peg against the USD. The CNY fixing rate against the USD was at 7.1 at the start of 2024, and has now drifted slightly higher to 7.1151 as of last weekend. In other words, Asian currencies like the yen and the Korean won have depreciated almost 12% and 6.5% respectively, while the Chinese currency has barely budged. Markets are growing nervous; the onshore traded USDCNY touched its highest levels of 2024 last week. And on March 22, when the PBoC fixing jumped to 7.1, the USDCNH ofshore market moved violently with USDCNH ending the day at 7.276.

We don’t expect a repeat of the second half of 2015, at least in our base case. The authorities have a much better handle on capital controls now, and large state banks have been the first line of defense for the PBoC, selling dollars on days when the CNY and CNH are under pressure. But there are definitely some similarities. In August 2015, markets reacted violently when the CNY depreciated just over 3% against the dollar. But for over 12 months before that, the CNY had been appreciating on a trade-weighted basis. China was running a quasi-peg against the USD and the greenback was on a run as investors positioned for a Fed hiking cycle, and hence so did the CNY. But when the PBoC tried to break away from this peg, investors reacted poorly and capital flight out of the country picked up.

Our baseline is that the PBoC will resist the pressure for a much weaker CNY until at least the November elections. A weaker Chinese currency at a time when Chinese exports are already in the crosshairs might lead to even more consternation in Western capitals. But if the USD goes on a new run – either because of stronger data or because large-scale tarifs seem more likely – it will become more dificult for China’s central bank to hold the line, as we discuss in more detail in 'Currency wars, China-style'.

A tail risk would be a substantial and unexpected depreciation in the CNY against the USD; China is such a large economy and such a giant exporter that a number of countries would feel forced to devalue to stay competitive. Too much strength in US data is a real problem, and far more for the rest of the world than the US.

## A reasonable baseline – with mostly downside risks

While trade wars, politics (French and US) and a too-strong dollar are concerns, we are less worried about geopolitics. Despite the war in the Middle East, oil has stayed remarkably wellbehaved. Even if it rises a bit from current levels, as our oil analyst forecasts, it is unlikely to change the macro outlook very much. The Russia-Ukraine war has taken a large toll on men and materiel in that part of the world, but stopped impacting financial markets on a regular basis a while ago.

All in all, when we consider the global outlook, the most likely path is for an unexciting expansion, uneven progress on inflation, and a slow easing cycle. That is a very acceptable outcome, considering that global hiking cycles are already over. But most risks to our baseline are admittedly skewed to the downside. Enjoy the quiet while it lasts, but with a wary eye on these tail scenarios.

Investment Outlook

## Reluctant longs

We are overweight global equities over fixed income for the third straight quarter. While still long risky assets, we are more cautious now, given elevated valuations and rising political and trade risks. Buying protection against many key tail risks is cheap; we outline a few key themes.

From mid-November last year, when the final Global Outlook of 2023 was published, we have consistently recommended overweighting global equities over global fixed income in this publication. It is a call that has become harder to hold on to, however. Stocks have gone on a massive run over the past seven months; the All-world index, the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq are all up about 25% in the past seven months. It is true that Big Tech earnings have continued to deliver. But political risks are rising (most recently in France), a global trade war looms, progress on inflation has been uneven, and the equity rally is being driven by an ever-shrinking group of just a few names. Being long equities is no longer an easy decision.

But it is even harder to love bonds at these levels. As noted above, progress on inflation has been uneven - in the US, euro area and UK. The BoJ is starting to get concerned about the efect of JPY depreciation and might have to pull back on QT. The US fiscal deficit is running at the same wide levels as last year. The snap elections in France have focused investor attention on how indebted the country (including households and corporates) is. Finally, global growth is not collapsing, which makes sharp easing cycles unlikely.

The macro backdrop still seems reasonable – with steady global growth, low jobless rates and an easing cycle finally starting. Yes, risks are rising and, yes, they are mostly tilted to the downside. But such scenarios do not strike us as very diferent from the usual 'wall of risks' that equity investors get paid to climb. And broad indices have been incredibly resilient for several quarters, even if it is mainly due to a few names. So we find ourselves yet again recommending overweighting global equities over fixed income this quarter. We might be reluctant longs, but longs we still are.

Below, we recommend a number of individual trade ideas across asset classes. Some reflect our overall macro view, others are pockets of opportunity we think are mispriced, and a couple are focused on non-linear payouts in the event of downside risk and reflect our bias to owning vol across asset classes.

## US rates likely to bounce back from the low end of the range as election risks come to the forefront

The US bond rally has now gone too far. The market is already priced for a significant easing cycle beyond 2024, especially considering that the neutral rate has been rising. Further cuts from the Fed hinge on inflation continuing to trend lower, but the chance of a massive cutting cycle is low, in our view. Further, we think that the US term premium has room to expand, and market focus is likely to shift back to fiscal and policy risks. We believe that the rally in yields

was sparked by unwinds of short-duration positions by leveraged investors, which were at fairly extreme levels going into the month. We maintain a short duration bias in the US, as a result.

Summer should be quiet on the US data and event front, which, together with the Fed’s guidance, should anchor near-term expectations on rates. However, fiscal uncertainty is likely to persist. But there is a lot of medium-term uncertainty regarding levels of longer-term rates because of uncertainty about neutral rates, the destabilizing efect of deficits, and the term premium. We prefer being long forward vol over the US elections in the form of selling 3m5y vol vs buying 1y5y vol; that term structure has flattened due to recent realized volatility, but could re-steepen.

## Rising US rates could again pose downside to risk assets

Higher rates have been associated with underperformance in risk assets in recent years whenever fiscal issues have come to the forefront. While large stock market sell-ofs would likely be in an environment where the Fed is forced to cut, a substantial sell-of could also be sparked by higher rates if fiscal and US monetary policy risks again come to the forefront. As an example, the SPX underperformed by nearly 10% between late July and late October last year, as Treasury yields rose nearly 100bp due to an upside surprise in US borrowing needs in early August. More recently, in April, a 50bp rise in yields was associated with the SPX falling nearly 5%. Conversely, the S&P 500 held up quite well post-SVB, despite a peak-to-trough rally of nearly 100bp in 5y yields.

The markets are not priced for a significantly positive correlation between bond prices and the S&P (or a negative correlation between rates and equities). While we are generally constructive on stocks, a digital put on the S&P to be down more than 5% by September in a scenario where 5y SOFR rates are higher than forwards ofers a payof of nearly 12:1, which is a cheap hedge for a potential repeat of last summer's market moves, in our view.

## France 10s30s steepeners for French election risks

Political uncertainty in France has taken the market by surprise over the past few weeks and sparked an unwind of carry trades across asset classes, including a widening in credit spreads and a cheapening of government bonds relative to swaps in Europe and the US. The spread between OATs and Bunds has widened to highs not reached in a decade, and there is a clear asymmetry towards wider spreads. We have long had a negative view on French government debt and have maintained 10y Spain-France tighteners as an expression of our bearish bias on OATs. The spread between 10y France and 10y Spain has compressed significantly, but we retain our negative bias, given that there could be a potential regime shift in OAT risks, depending on some of the more fiscally negative elections scenarios.

One interesting side efect of the sharp repricing of France in recent weeks was that it was led by futures' underperformance. This has led to a flattening of the 10s30s curve. At 40bp, the France 10s30s curve is now at the flattest levels of the year, substantially lower than the steepest level of about 60bp in January. During previous events when French sovereign risk was a market focus (such as the 2011-13 European sovereign crisis and France-specific worries in 2016-17), 10s30s France steepened much more because the fiscal risk premium generally tends to get priced more at the long end of the curve. We expect a reversal of the recent flattening in the France 10s30s curve, especially if election outcomes are negative.

## The next bottleneck in AI data center infrastructure

Megacap tech stocks have been increasing capex more than 40% y/y, and this ramp-up provides opportunity in sub-themes, especially where capacity bottlenecks exist. It is perhaps still not as widely appreciated that computing is only half of this capex spend and areas such as data center land and construction capex make up the other half. Within data center build-outs, AI has unsurprisingly emerged as an exceptionally fast-growing segment. According to the IEA (2024), data centers could use up to twice as much electricity by 2026, driven largely by AI and cryptocurrencies. The IEA has calculated that data centers, cryptocurrencies and AI accounted for nearly 2% of total global electricity demand in 2022, using 460TWh of electricity, and this combined demand is estimated to reach 620-1050 TWh in 2026 – the equivalent of adding another Sweden or Germany to total demand. According to our own aggregated single-stock estimates, the power consumption of AI data centers alone is likely to jump 17x from 2022 to 2026.

Markets caught onto this theme this quarter by indiscriminately bidding up power utility stocks across DMs (VRT US +76% YTD vs. SPX, for instance) along with, more recently, renewable stocks. While power generation will remain a key bottleneck, we believe that if the AI demand is to sustain, the rally will need to broaden into other sub-segments such as data center interconnect (ie, the movement of data from one compute/switching node to another), where the concentration of spend has increased to 30% of server BOM from 10% in traditional deployments. The need for high speed, lossless, and directly-connected accelerators needed for the training of LLMs has presented bottlenecks, with the interconnect ecosystem being forced to innovate on more of a yearly cadence vs. historical multiyear cycles. It has also given rise to new types of deployments, eg, the 'back end', which enable accelerator-to-accelerator connections. As compute demands grow, this will ultimately require larger compute nodes and therefore 'clustering', which will also require further densification of interconnect. We believe the names enabling more eficient interconnect in segments such as module OEMs, analog and DSP providers will drive the second wave of AI investments and are still under-priced for the opportunity set (especially given the run-up in power utilities).

## Buy UKX up-and-out calls

Regardless of the outcome of the UK general elections on 4 July, we see policy continuity amid limited fiscal room as an anchor to keep volatility and left-tail risks contained. Moreover, as polls currently suggest, a potential Labor win would likely boost both equities and sterling, with the market pricing in a re-convergence of the UK with Europe, unwinding some of the pound’s Brexit premium and the prospect of a more stable government.

UKX ofers value trading at a forward multiple of 11.7x (vs. the SPX and NKY at 22.5x and 22.1x), with fundamentals that justify a re-rating. Record long-term outflows since Brexit have also left investors deeply under-positioned: MSCI UK's market value is at its lowest ever as a percentage of MSCI World, at 4% vs. an average of 10% in the 1990s. Moreover, UK equities could benefit from TINA flows within Europe as political (and even fiscal) uncertainty rises across the latter. Given that UKX call skew is at the flattest levels in nearly 20 years, we like owning call spreads or up-and-out calls on UKX as well as UKX calls contingent on lower EURGBP (see rationale for EURGBP below). For instance, UKX 3M 8300-8700 up-and-out call costs \~65bps indicatively (ref: 8144, \~60% cheapening vs vanilla call).

## Go long USDCNH via 6 month forwards

The surprising rebound of the Chinese economy in Q1 hid elements of front-loading in policy stimulus. During this period, private sector demand remained exceptionally anemic. With growth targets now in reach, the second half of the year will likely entail a marked slowdown in activity to a sequential pace below 4%. Beyond the obvious feedback loop between growth, rates and FX, government plans for the absorption of the lingering real estate shock will also necessitate easy monetary conditions in China.

What is more, as we discuss in "Currency wars, China-style," real exchange rate depreciation has been an integral part of China's industrial policy to boost competitiveness and gain market share. That policy has started to reverse, owing to the stable CNY to the dollar. The self-

defeating nature of this stability is likely to make the CNY vulnerable, especially in an environment where outright competitors such as the yen depreciate markedly.

CNY weakness will likely loop back into a stronger dollar and back into CNH. This is due not only to the standard pass-through of CNH moves to EUR, which tend to be large whenever fundamental drivers are involved. It is also due to the potential efect of slower China growth on European PMIs. Against a backdrop of US exceptionalism and resilient growth, this may be the catalyst for a bigger and broader dollar rally that feeds back into Asian FX.

## From BReturn to FRexit: Go short EURGBP

The political tables could not have turned more decisively for these two economies. In early July, France and the UK will face critical parliamentary elections. In France, the totality of Le Pen’s policies and the higher likelihood of a government in which her party is the key decision maker may amount to an erosion of credibility for EU institutions, policy conflict with EU institutions and other EU states, and an escalation of tensions in Russia-Ukraine. None of this is positive for the single currency, nor has it built a significant risk premium. As we noted recently, a 2016-style widening of France-Germany spreads could bring EUR crosses lower by several percentage points.

Across the English channel, the change of political leadership in the UK brings (among other things) the prospect for a change in its stance with regard to international trade treaties and, more significantly, a rapprochement with the EU. The way the Brexit process ultimately played out amounted to three supply-side shocks (availability of trade, price of trade and labour mobility), which spilled over to investment, capital flows and FX performance. Most notably, the GBP has traded in a visible discount since 2016. A reversal of trade and mobility barriers, even a partial one can help improve underlying FX fundamentals and valuations.

## Summary of Asset Allocation Themes

## Equities

## Key Forecasts

• In the US, we maintain our base case YE 24 S&P 500 price target at 5300, based on 22.5x our FY24 EPS estimate of \$235. While Big Tech has raised the floor for S&P 500 earnings this year, our below-consensus EPS forecast is based on expectations for margin pressure to afect earnings for the rest of the index. In our bull case scenario, in which ex-Tech EPS revisions have bottomed and Big Tech captures an even higher forward valuation based on continued momentum in upward revisions, the S&P 500 could go to 6050, based on 24x our bull case FY24 EPS estimate of \$252. We think risks are tilted to the upside for the S&P 500.

• Near term, we downgraded our tactical OW view on European equities to MW after the French election was called. Positioning looks quite long; summer seasonality is about to kick in; and elections not just in France, but the UK and US as well, are adding to uncertainty. Near-term price action could therefore be choppier, but medium term we still believe ‘Goldilocks-ish’ data, range-bound yields, and resilient earnings continue to favour equities as an asset class. With volatility so low, we recommend using cheap optionality for equity replacement and/or downside hedges. Valuations remain undemanding outside of Tech/Quality/Growth. Our price targets are 545 for Euro Stoxx (SXXE) and 8300 for FTSE 100 (UKX), implying low to midsingle-digit upside into year-end.

## Key Recommendations

• After 18 months of Big Tech dominance, we think the group has more room to run over the next couple of quarters, even though the bar to deliver has been set very high. Big Tech revisions have strengthened further post-Q1 earnings, bifurcating even more from the rest of the S&P 500. Big Tech's defensible margins have scarcity value in an environment characterized by slowing growth and sticky inflation. Outside of Big Tech, we are constructive on Growth stocks, based on superior earnings upside ofsetting the near-term headwind from elevated real rates. Similarly, Momentum and Size are well positioned for EPS and margin upside. We also favor Quality to manage downside risk. We are neutral on Value, which tends to benefit from higher real rates, with the assumption that the Fed will deliver a first cut sometime in 2H24.

• We have also made adjustments to our sector allocation, taking some of the reflationary stance of the table. We have downgraded Energy from OW to UW on faltering oil prices and downgraded Autos to UW. On the flip side, we have upgraded both Staples and Utilities from UW to MW, partly to close defensive UW’s, but also to reduce the UW long-duration sector exposure they brought, as rate-cutting cycle gets going in Europe. We have also added to consumer exposure by raising Retail to OW and added some long-duration exposure by taking Real Estate to OW.

## Bonds

## Key Forecasts

• We expect Treasury yields to rise over the coming quarter as increasing real income growth and household net worth imply that the consensus is too negative on the economy. Markets have already priced in a material easing cycle, with the real rate expected to fall well below the estimated short-run real neutral rate, which is not justified by the inflation progress alone. The term premium has fallen to levels not justified by fundamentals, and forced unwinds of short-duration positions by leveraged investors have exacerbated the rally.

• Outright euro yields have been trading with a bearish bias through most of H1, reflecting bearish spillovers from the US, upside growth surprises and sticky services inflation. Bunds have experienced a sharp flight-to-quality rally since French President Macron called for snap elections: further escalation of political worries could drive a fresh rally, whereas easing of these fears could drive a relief sell-of. In our baseline forecasts, we see 10y Bund yields at 2.45% in Q4 24 and 2.35% in Q2 25.

## Key Recommendations

• We recommended shorting 10y US Treasuries to position for higher rates over the coming quarter. We also recommend 2y swap spread wideners after the tightening move in response to political uncertainty in France, as the funding backdrop remains benign. We maintain a 3m5y vs. 1y5y vol calendar spread to position for near-term anchoring versus longer-term risks, given that the vol term structure is flattest in 5y tenors.

• At the front end, we hold ERH5 longs, with c.65bp of cuts priced across the next six ECB meetings (versus 100bp of cuts in our economists’ baseline). Further out on the curve, we hold 10s30s 1yf steepeners to position for an eventual leg of long-end curve steepening with relatively benign carry dynamics in the interim (10s30s 1yf only 1bp higher than spot). In EGBs, we hold longs in 10y Spain vs. France, given relatively strong Spanish fundamentals alongside ongoing French political turbulence. In the UK, we hold a cross-market long in 30y Gilts vs BTPs, given the cheapness of long Gilt forwards and expectations of relatively orthodox post-election fiscal policy in the UK.

## Commodities

## Key Forecasts

• Oil prices have rebounded from what seemed to us be an overreaction to the OPEC+ meeting last week to the range they had been in since early May, when we argued for a grind higher as the baseline scenario. We have not seen this play out so far, but implied volatility has continued to decline and the options-implied probability of tail scenarios on both sides has compressed, while that of a small move higher has increased more than that of a small move lower, implying greater confidence in our baseline scenario. The distribution is still skewed to the left overall, given relatively soft demand indicators of late, but less so than early last month.

• The IEA has revised down its 2024 global demand growth forecast to less than 1 mb/d, compared with 1.3 mb/d in March, but given the upward revision to the  baseline estimate for last year, their absolute demand forecast of 103.2 for this year is close to our 103.3 mb/d forecast. Even if H2 demand is 0.2 mb/d below our forecast, inventories should still draw through the end of the year, especially given the recent OPEC+ announcement, which tightens our balance expectations for H2 by 0.5 mb/d, all else equal.

## Key Recommendations

• Oil prices are depressed relative to our \$90/b fair value estimate for Brent, which makes us stick to our short put spreads recommendation.

## Inflation

## Key Forecasts

• We view the risks to the US inflation outlook, especially from Owners’ Equivalent Rent, as tilted to the upside relative to the market, which is priced for target-consistent inflation in the short, medium, and longer term. Against this backdrop, we believe structural long breakeven positions across the curve ofer cheap inflation insurance. Technical headwinds, such as growing supply, a stagnant demand base, an expected turn down in carry, and perceptions of a credible Fed, are pushing against near-term prospects, in our view, and we recommend waiting for a better entry point to initiate new breakeven trades.

• EA inflation markets remain priced in line with or modestly above ECB target-consistent outcomes across the forecast horizon. As EA disinflation progress remains slow, we think the HICPx market pricing will remain range-bound. In the UK, we look for a normalization of the forward inflation risk premium vs. the BoE forecast. We also see UK real duration as reasonably attractive, given relatively cheap valuations and limited near-term fiscal and political risks.

## Key Recommendations

• Long OAT€i43 on fly vs. OAT€i34 and OAT€i53.

• Short 2yfwd1y RPI; long IL37/44 forward real yield.

## Credit

## Key Forecasts

• In the US, we expect spreads to trade at 85-90bp in IG and 290-315bp in HY by YE, ie, at about current levels. While positive seasonal technicals might push spreads tighter in the summer, we believe it will be hard for them to stay there for a significant time, as it would require a meaningful change in the market's assessment of the CRE risk implications for financials in IG and of TMT-related issues in lower-quality HY.

• In Europe, the combination of a slowly improving macro backdrop, the ECB on an easing path and strong demand technicals given high all-in yields should allow IG spreads to tighten further, while HY is likely to be range-bound. The key risks are US-driven rate volatility, China (growth downside and tarif escalation), and in the short term the French election outcome.

## Key Recommendations

• In US IG, we prefer the front end and high-quality low-\$ price in the long end. We believe that BBBs have compressed too much relative to As. In US Lev Fin, we continue to prefer Lev Loans over HY bonds. In HY, we find Bs particularly tight, whereas BBs seem more fairly valued vs. BBBs. If the market is to rally further, it will have to be through CCCs.

• In European IG, we prefer the 5y point, and we still prefer financials to non-financials, but upin-rating in financials and down-in-rating in non-cyclicals. In Lev Fin, we prefer leveraged loans to HY bonds. Within HY, we like subordinated bonds over seniors. We like AT1s over Brated credit and insurance hybrids over bank LT2s and corporate hybrids.

## Emerging Markets

## Key Recommendations

Amid steady global growth but sticky inflation, markets await more clarity on the path of US rates. In the meantime, idiosyncratic developments are fuelling volatility in EM assets. We expect limited beta conviction and continued issuer diferentiation to prevail in EM credit, while EM rates should remain range-bound.

• EM credit: Our high-conviction OWs in EM HY sovereigns include Ghana and Sri Lanka, on our expectations that the respective restructuring processes can be successfully concluded soon. In the performing EM HY sector, we rate Nigeria, Egypt and Ecuador OW and have upgraded

Kenya and the Dominican Republic to OW recently. Conversely, we remain UW on South Africa and Argentina. Among IG sovereigns, valuations look attractive on aggregate, but the sector faces substantial headwinds from rates volatility and some idiosyncratic developments. We rate Mexico UW and have downgraded KSA to UW. The CEE region could be a potential target for global USD IG investors, given attractive valuations, but contagion from the political volatility in the euro area may be a hurdle in the near term. We expect EEMEA/LatAm corporates to outperform sovereigns further and recommend focusing on lower-rated rated segments in Asia, especially in India HY corporates (OW).

• EM FX/Local: Higher for longer US rates, low levels of forward EM vs. US forward rates and an unfavourable environment for EM central bank cuts are creating dificult trade-ofs for EM fixed income. We are focusing mostly on local/idiosyncratic stories such as China, Israel and South Africa.

## Foreign Exchange

## Key Forecasts

• The operational level of the economy, the political risks, the skewed market pricing, the efect of coming US elections and the likely slowdown in China’s growth in H2 all favour a stronger dollar, especially vs. G10 but also vs. Asia. We have also downgraded our forecasts for a number of key EMs.

## Key Recommendations

• We recommend short EURGBP, as a potential closer link between the UK and the EU should reduce the Brexit premium in the GBP.

• We prefer to express our bullish view via long USDCNH 6m forwards, as fundamentals and carry benefit.

• The European political turmoil makes EUR our preferable funder and poses even more downside in CEE currencies via the Russian link; hence, we continue to recommend short EURILS spot and long EURHUF spot.

• Key RV trades include short CNHINR 3m NDF and short THBKRW 3m NDF.

Commodities views published in Energy Sigma: Reset, 14 June 2024. Inflation views published in Even a stopped clock, 13 June 2024 and Weight, Weight... Do Tell Me! 23 May 2024. Credit views published in Resigned to the rally, 31 May 2024, and La volatilité est de retour, 14 June 2024. Emerging Market views published in The Emerging Markets Quarterly, Waiting isn't easy, 19 June 2024. Foreign Exchange views published in Of underpriced divergence and rising political risks, 18 June 2024.

## US Immigration

## A mighty tailwind

Surging US immigration has helped boost economic growth, reduce inflation, and fuel expectations of monetary easing. With policy measures threatening to upend the status quo, we quantify immigration flows and assess likely efects on the outlook under alternative scenarios.

• US net immigration reached a record high of 470k in December 2023, and a total of 4mn in 2023 as a whole. Steady immigration flows have been a key source of US exceptionalism over the post-pandemic period, providing a strong tailwind for aggregate supply that has helped sustain disinflation amid a robust, consumer-fueled expansion.

• We introduce the Barclays Immigration Tracker, a monthly estimate of US net immigration since early 2014, combining data from 14 oficial data sources.

• Our dataset shows that US immigration has been fueled by a surge in humanitarian immigrants, whose contribution to total immigration flows surged from 16% in 2014 to more than 50% in 2023. The majority of these are asylum seekers, who qualify for a work permit after at least a 180-day period under US immigration law. Our data indicate that most are young adults and families that arrive through the Southwest US border from a shifting mix of countries in Central and South America.

• Although this surge is politically unpopular, it has had many desirable macroeconomic consequences, helping to relieve post-pandemic labor shortages and boost aggregate supply. We estimate that net immigration increased the US population by 3.2mn in 2022, and the labor force by 2.1mn. In 2023, these gains accelerated to 4.0mn and 2.5mn, respectively.

• While immigrants hold a little over one fifth of all jobs, we estimate they accounted for about 75% of the increase in private payroll employment over the past year. Moreover, hours worked by immigrants contributed to nearly a third of output growth over that period, more than ofsetting a drag from hours worked by the US-born, with the remainder explained by productivity growth.

• Policies to stem the flow of asylum seekers will squeeze labor supply and growth. According to our calculations, sustaining the Biden administration's recent executive action would eventually trim monthly payroll gains by about 65k/m in 2025, while a return to 2017-18 policies, enacted during the Trump administration, would subtract about 125k/m.
