## Introduction

The US economy has outperformed its peers over the post-pandemic period, achieving strong output growth despite easing inflationary pressures. Instead of going into recession last year, as many analysts had expected following the Fed's aggressive rate hikes in 2022, the economy grew 3.1% Q4/Q4 in 2023. Even more surprisingly, this came amid rapid disinflation and slowing wage growth, with core PCE prices decelerating from 5.6% Q4/Q4 in 2022 to 3.1% Q4/Q4 in 2023.

## Immigration has provided a mighty tailwind...

In our view, the recent surge in immigration into the US has been a key contributor to this dynamic, with rapid increases in labor supply and potential GDP allowing strong economic growth to coexist with diminished inflationary pressures. In retrospect, these efects of strong immigration were likely a significant contributor enabling the FOMC's decision to end its hiking cycle in July 2023, in turn opening the door for a transition in focus from sustaining tight policy to considering the appropriateness of potential rate cuts. Indeed, even with payroll employment continuing to post robust gains, labor-market indicators generally point to a diminished imbalance between supply and demand, with rates of job openings, hires, separations, and quits having all normalized into the vicinity of pre-pandemic levels. All of these developments suggest that immigration has supplemented monetary policy through its efects on labor supply.

## ...but these winds are now shifting direction

To be sure, immigration has also been the source of substantial political fallout. This reflects not only discomfort with the rapid influx of migrants, but also economic tensions from erosion in real wage growth and intensified shortages of housing. Indeed, both the Democrats and the Republicans are campaigning on policies to slow immigration flows. In early June, the Biden administration issued a presidential order that attempts to stem the flow of asylum seekers at the southern border, while limits to undocumented immigration have been a key plank of the Republican policy agenda for some time now.

Against this backdrop, in this piece, we aim to quantify the efects of immigration and its impact on labor supply, and to assess the macroeconomic efects of changes in immigration policy. The first of these tasks is quite challenging due to measurement dificulties, the ever-changing drivers of migration flows, and shifts in the policy regime. To this end, we comb through the data sources to construct a monthly Barclays Immigration Tracker. Among other things, this quantification suggests that net immigration lifted the US labor force by about 2.5mn in 2023, and that – even with Biden's presidential action – we are on pace to see a similar addition to the workforce this year after allowing for the six-to-nine-month delay needed to obtain work authorizations.

We then use information from our tracker database to estimate efects on the supply of labor and potential output.<sup>2</sup> We find that while immigrants hold a little over one fifth of all jobs, they accounted for about 75% of the increase in private payroll employment over the past year. Moreover, hours worked by immigrants contributed to nearly a third of output growth, more than ofsetting a drag from hours worked by the US-born, with the remainder explained by productivity growth.

We work through how alternative policy scenarios might afect the medium-term macroeconomic outlook. These alternatives include:

• A 'binding' scenario in which the Biden administration's executive action works as advertised and is sustained through 2025.

• A 'business as usual' scenario where it proves inefective.

• A 'severe' scenario where the US returns to the strict immigration controls of 2017-18, under the Trump administration.<sup>3</sup>

Our estimates suggest that relative to the 'business as usual' scenario, the Biden administration's executive action (ie, the 'binding' scenario) could reduce monthly flows into payroll employment by about 65k/m by end-2025, while controls of the sort implemented during the Trump presidency in 2017-18 would reduce flows by twice this magnitude.

The next section describes briefly the Barclays Immigration Tracker, the diferent types of immigrants, and our approach to estimating net and gross flows. We relegate the details to the Appendix. The section after that discusses recent immigration trends for the major categories of immigrants and provides insights on those categories. In the rest of the report, we examine the macroeconomic implications of the immigration surge, focusing on potential payroll gains and potential output growth, in the three scenarios mentioned above.

## Introducing the Barclays Immigration Tracker

Computing net immigration flows is an extremely challenging task for a number of reasons. First, and foremost, there is no centralized immigration data source – data are dispersed across a large number of platforms and government agencies' websites. Depending on the source, data have a diferent format (some track unique individuals, while others track number of entries) and are available at diferent frequencies (eg, monthly, quarterly or annual; calendar year vs fiscal year). Furthermore, there is no consensus on either the number or the definition of immigration categories.

In this report, we address these issues and develop a methodology to compute net immigration flows. The output of this methodology is a new database of net immigration flows, which is available at a monthly frequency and goes back to January 2014. We call this dataset the Barclays Immigration Tracker.

We define five immigration categories: humanitarian immigrants, unlawful entries, overstays, green cards granted abroad and temporary resident visa holders.

At a high level, our approach follows three steps:

1. First, we collect gross immigration data (people entering the US) for each of these five categories by combining data from 14 diferent sources (see Figure 19 in the Appendix), including visa issuance from the Department of State, admissions data from the Department of Homeland Security, encounters and releases from the Customs and Border Protection.

2. Then, we carefully adjust our gross estimates to avoid double-counting, for example, individuals who change immigration status (eg, from temporary resident visa holders to green card holders) or individuals who enter and exit the US multiple times (eg, temporary resident visa holders who go on vacation outside of the US).

3. Finally, we subtract emigration flows (people who permanently move out of the US) from the gross flows to arrive at monthly net immigration flows.

FIGURE 1. Computing gross and net immigration flows, 2023
![](images/2d26ba72e6bc2a5c7a80eb6449d03aa73b1949010477f9b740ca106b58e619c8.jpg)
Note: The areas of the circles are proportional to the immigration flows they represent. Based on 2023 data. Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Socia Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research

## Five immigration categories

For more details on our methodology, refer to the Appendix.

We believe our five categories are detailed enough such that we can uncover interesting trends in the data, but at the same time broad enough that they allow us to keep the analysis simple.

The first three categories (humanitarian immigrants, unlawful entries and overstays) are commonly referred to as 'unauthorized' immigration,<sup>4</sup> while the remaining two categories (green cards granted abroad and temporary resident visa holders) are often described as 'documented' or 'legal' immigration. As an example, in Figure 1 (top panel) we show gross immigration flows for 2023 for each of the five categories, where the areas of the circles are proportional to the size of the immigration flows they represent.

• Humanitarian immigrants are by far the most relevant category to understanding the current US immigration landscape. Their number has been rising since 2021, and in 2023 alone, we estimate that 2.2mn humanitarian immigrants entered the US. The majority can be described as either asylum seekers or migrants under various humanitarian or special programs (eg, programs specifically designed for nationals of certain countries, including Ukraine, Cuba, and Haiti, or family reunification programs). Without exception, these individuals are stopped by Customs and Border Protection (CBP) oficers upon arriving to the US because they do not meet the usual legal requirements (ie, they attempt to enter without paperwork). However, some are later released and allowed to enter the US through the use of asylum claims, parole or other special notices, which often allow them to seek employment. We discuss humanitarian immigrants in greater detail in A primer on humanitarian immigration.

• Unlawful entries are individuals who avoid apprehension or contact with CBP oficers when they enter the US. Some of them are detected, but never stopped, while others are never detected. We estimate that a total of 0.9mn people unlawfully entered the US in 2023.<sup>5</sup> The

overwhelming majority of detected unlawful entries occur at the Southwest border (98% of the total detected entries), with the rest occurring either by sea or through the Northern border.

• Overstays are individuals who were lawfully admitted to the US on a temporary status, but who remained in the country after their legal standing had expired. Approximately 90% of the total of 0.4mn overstays (as of 2023) originally entered the US as tourists or business travelers.

• Green cards granted abroad refers to immigrants with a permanent resident status, who have the right to enter and live in the US on a green card.<sup>6</sup> We only consider green cards granted abroad and exclude those issued to people already residing in the US under a diferent immigration status, because these individuals do not represent new arrivals (ie, they are already accounted for in one of our other categories). In 2023, 0.7mn people arrived in the US on green cards granted abroad.

• Temporary resident visa holders are individuals who can only reside in the country for the duration of their visa status, such as students or temporary workers (who make up more than 75% of this category). For the purposes of our analysis, we exclude short-term visitors (eg, tourists, business travelers, crew members and people in transit), since they are not expected to reside or work in the country. Gross immigration flows for temporary resident visa holders (2.3mn in 2023) are at par with the humanitarian immigrants category, but we note that this is also the most dynamic category, with a large number of people moving out of the country.

## From gross to net immigration

Our next step is to compute emigration flows (people leaving the US) for each of the five categories. For the two legal immigration categories (green cards granted abroad and temporary resident visa holders) the process relies on actual emigration data. For the other immigration categories (humanitarian immigrants, unlawful entries and overstays), we rely on estimates that are based on a combination of forced removals<sup>7</sup> from the US (ie, deportations) and voluntary emigration flows.

We distinguish between two types of voluntary emigration rates – a short-term emigration rate that we apply to the gross flow of unauthorized immigrants and a long-term rate that we apply to the stock of the unauthorized population.<sup>8</sup> Following the prior literature,<sup>9</sup> we apply a 3.2% short-term emigration rate on unlawful entries and overstays, and a 1.6% rate on the humanitarian immigrants. We assume that humanitarian immigrants have a substantially lower incentive to leave the US for two reasons: first, these individuals are typically forced to flee their home countries to avoid political persecution or economic hardship; and second, they tend to immigrate with their entire families, including children. We find strong support for both of these assumptions in the data (see Figure 17 and Figure 18). Following a similar logic, we assume that voluntary long-term emigration only applies to unlawful entries and overstays, but not to humanitarian immigrants, and use a rate of 2.5% to derive these long-term emigration flows.

Finally, we compute net immigration flows as the diference between the gross immigration and the sum of deportations and estimated emigration flows. As an example, Figure 1 (bottom panel) shows the resulting net flows for 2023, when we estimate that a total of 2.5mn people emigrated from the US. To conserve space, in the sections that follow, any reference to immigration reflects net estimates.

## US immigration reached record highs in 2023 fueled by a surge in humanitarian immigrants

## A surge in humanitarian immigrants

In Figure 2 we show the Barclays Immigration Tracker net immigration flows at an annual frequency, split by immigration category. Between 2014 and 2019, immigration remained relatively stable, with flows oscillating around 1mn per year. The most drastic reduction took place in 2020, when pandemic-era restrictions limited cross-border mobility, and as a result, net immigration in 2020 was negative (ie, emigration surpassed immigration). Since these restrictions were lifted in 2021, immigration flows have risen steadily, reaching an all-time high of 4mn in 2023.

FIGURE 2. Barclays Immigration Tracker, annual series
![](images/b88dc007d71ebb1877e230e376a168c8d03858936a7830c884e4c7069d72cd69.jpg)
Note: Number labels show total net flows. 2024 data available through April.
Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Social Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research

The data show the increase in net immigration was driven mostly by the rise in humanitarian immigrants and, to a lesser degree, unlawful entries. The number of humanitarian immigrants (see A primer on humanitarian immigration) began to rise in the post-Covid era, when mobility restrictions were lifted, and by 2023, humanitarian immigrants became the largest source of net immigration to the US. In 2023, this category accounted for 2.2mn immigrants, which represented 54% of the total net immigration (Figure 4). For comparison, humanitarian immigrants accounted for just 16% of net immigration in 2014 (Figure 3).

FIGURE 3. Net immigration, 2014

![](images/344268d2864b31e5dfa5af1e917d65abac6e3eaf499e329c56b6ea9fd8c61238.jpg)
FIGURE 4. Net immigration, 2023

![](images/56d0033e158d18b9181ef947d9794243a53c360ee989564cb7f3809fcca96ffe.jpg)
Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Social Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research
Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Social Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research

In Figure $^ { 5 , }$ we show the Barclays Immigration Tracker net immigration flows on a monthly basis. The number of humanitarian immigrants reached a peak in December 2023, when 312k individuals entered the US. In 2024, net flows have come down markedly from that high-water mark and have stabilized around 200k individuals per month.

FIGURE 5. Barclays Immigration Tracker, monthly series

![](images/336f20d9189f39e79ab1663bcabab041a6604ac12f5e374f1b9e056ce9cb7627.jpg)

Note: Temporary resident visa holders series presents seasonally-adjusted estimates using an X-13 filter. 2024 data through April.

Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Social Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research

Compared to our Barclays Immigration Tracker, the Congressional Budget Ofice (CBO), which made immigration projections in November 2023, showed lower projections for that year, at 3.3mn. However, these projections did not incorporate the record-high immigration surge in December 2023. Further, the CBO only provides data annually and groups immigration data for humanitarian immigrants, unlawful entries and overstays in the same category. Our tracker is available monthly and disaggregates data into more narrowly defined categories, which allows us to identify diferent trends across groups in a timelier way and to estimate more accurately the contribution of immigration to the labor market.

## Macroeconomic implications of the immigration surge

The post-pandemic surge in immigration has likely been one of the key factors behind the resilient macro outlook, helping to ease the tight labor-supply conditions that prevailed following the pandemic and, at the same time, providing some direct reinforcement for aggregate demand. In the absence of this influence, we think that labor shortages would have been a more meaningful headwind. This might have exacerbated wage and price pressures and, hence, required a more restrictive monetary policy stance.

With this in mind, this section attempts to quantify the efects on potential employment and potential output of the recent immigration surge, then works through alternative scenarios about how these inflows might play out over the medium term.

We find that since 2022, when job vacancies reached record levels, jobs have increasingly been filled by immigrants, so that immigration significantly contributed to boosting employment gains in 2023 and so far in 2024. We estimate that in the 12 months ending in April 2024, about 75% of all private nonfarm payroll jobs gains were filled by immigrants, and nearly one third of output growth was attributable to working immigrants, with most of the rest accounted for by productivity growth. This stands in sharp contrast with the pre-pandemic period, when nearly 80% of the job gains were filled by US-born.

Going forward, we find that slower immigration flows are likely to exert a drag on potential jobs gains and potential output growth. To the extent this will contribute to renewed labor market tightness, it may possibly put upward pressure on rates, all else equal.

## Immigrant workers helped boost the US labor market

We use the Barclays Immigration Tracker to quantify the impact of immigration on the supply of labor. Depending on the immigration category, we use one of two approaches to compute the contribution of (net) immigration to labor supply. For temporary resident visa holders, the process is straightforward because we can use the visa category (eg, H1B) to identify the number of individuals who are allowed to work.<sup>10</sup> For the remaining four categories, we use a two-step approach. First, we compute the share of working-age adults (ie, we exclude children and seniors) and then apply the same foreign-born civilian labor participation rates (both formal and informal sectors) from the Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS). More details can be found in the Appendix. For example, we estimate that around 60% of all temporary resident visa holders and green cards holders join the labor force, while that number is close to 70% for all humanitarian immigrants. We estimate the labor force participation rate of all immigrant adults to be around 78%.

## Immigration added 2.5mn to the US labor market in 2023

Figure 6 shows the contribution to labor supply for each of the five net immigration categories. In general, the data follow the same trend over time as net immigration flows. This not surprising since both the share of adults and the labor participation rates are relatively stable over the period we study. In total, we estimate that net immigration boosted labor supply by 2.5mn in 2023, surpassing the 2.1mn in 2022. In the first four months of 2024, we estimate that net immigration added 0.8mn to the labor force, retaining a pace similar to that of 2023, and surpassing in four months the contributions to labor force estimated for the full years through 2021.

FIGURE 6. Contribution of immigration to labor supply
![](images/79cdba456f986a3ebdadd6a43798953e5b6ad1176de00f70f21b849a897a8709.jpg)
Note: Number labels show total net flows. 2024 data through April.
Source: US Department of Homeland Security, US Department of State, US Customs and Border Protection, US Socia Security Administration, Congressional Budget Ofice, TRAC Immigration, Barclays Research

## Immigration’s contribution to private nonfarm payroll employment has grown

While the BLS publishes data on foreign-born employment in the monthly Current Population Survey (CPS), this series is based on a small sample and sufers from increasingly stale population weights over the course of a given calendar year, as well as large discrete realignments and compositional changes from sampling variation.<sup>11</sup>

Given the broad-based interest in the evolution of nonfarm payroll employment (NFP), its great reliability, and its importance in determining US output growth, we attempt to quantify the contribution of immigration to this measure of employment. Immigrants included in NFP numbers wish to work (they are part of the labor force) and likely have received a work permit. Consequently, we consider workers who have entered the country as green card holders, temporary resident visa holders, or humanitarian immigrants. Importantly, we do not include individuals who entered the country unlawfully or people overstaying (we assume that their work authorization expires in that case). To do so, we use the data constructed by the Barclays Immigration Tracker introduced above, as we need to distinguish among types of immigrants; we cannot use the CBO's estimates, which group immigration data for humanitarian immigrants, unlawful entries and overstays in the same category and provide only annual data.

We focus on the private sector, essentially assuming that incoming immigrants, by and large, do not work for the government. Moreover, to capture immigrants to the private nonfarm sector, we remove from the monthly flow of immigrants entering the labor force, those going into the agricultural sector.<sup>12</sup> Given that humanitarian immigrants are likely to get authorization to work at least six months after having entered the country, we count as part of the addition to current labor force, humanitarian immigrants who entered the country six months prior. Also, to be

included in NFP, immigrants must have found a job; that is, they are not unemployed.<sup>13</sup> Finally, since workers can in principle have more than one job, we scale up the number of immigrants in the labor force to convert it into jobs.<sup>14</sup>

Figure 7 shows the evolution of immigrant employment according to three diferent measures. The grey line captures the number of jobs held by foreign-born employees from the CPS, scaled up to account for multiple jobs holders. The blue line represents our estimate of private NFP jobs held by immigrants with work permits (initialized at the level of the grey line in March 2014). It lies below the grey line in recent years, as it excludes workers in government and the agricultural sector, and those with no work authorization, among others.<sup>15</sup>

In other words, our estimated series of private NFP attributed to immigrants is narrowly defined and provides a fairly conservative estimate of the contribution of immigrants to employment. For comparison, the green line shows our estimate of the potential number of jobs held by immigrants under a more expansive definition – the potential immigrant labor force including those without work authorization (also initialized at the CPS level of March 2014). As the chart makes clear, the diference between our measure of private NFP of immigrants and that broader measure has expanded by about 4mn over the past 10 years.

FIGURE 7. Employment of foreign-born workers has been increasing rapidly in recent years
![](images/a3b84746eacc9836e1b05a5e0004719cd58fe6a70ae175341d7b79ed743b8892.jpg)
The grey line captures the number of jobs held by foreign-born employees from the CPS, scaled up for multiple jobs holders. The blue line represents our estimate of private nonfarm payroll jobs held by immigrants with work authorizations. The green line shows our estimate of the potential number of jobs held by immigrants with or without authorization (initialized at CPS level of March 2014).
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Figure 8 shows that, despite the recent rapid increase of private-sector jobs held by immigrants, their share in the level of overall employment has remained little changed over the past decade (outside of the pandemic), accounting now for about 23% of all private NFP employment, with the remainder 77% held by the US-born.<sup>16</sup>

FIGURE 8. Immigrants account for about 23% of overall private nonfarm payrolls
![](images/6ff0255dedb95e24e3c937768ec64414a25f60f978edf59379fc807e75c2d7b1.jpg)
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Nonetheless, we estimate that private payroll gains have increasingly been filled by immigrants in recent years. As shown in Figure 9, immigrants contributed only a little to monthly payroll gains from 2015 to 2021. In particular, in the five years preceding the pandemic, private NFP gains averaged 180k/m, with 145k/m (or 80%) filled by the US-born and only about 35k/m by immigrants.

Since early 2022, with job vacancies reaching a record high of over 12mn, much of the job openings posted by businesses have been filled by immigrants. By April 2024, the situation had flipped compared to the pre-pandemic period: private NFP job gains were averaging 181k/m (12mma), of which we estimate 136k/m (or 75%) were filled by immigrants, while the US-born added only 44k jobs per month, on average. (See Figure 12 below.)

FIGURE 9. Since 2022, with record job openings, jobs have increasingly been filled by immigrants
![](images/f68645b41a823da3e55ef8c60dc01765df9fe5f4e2aadc2c5349e9e2fc617b72.jpg)
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Looking ahead: Employment gains likely continue to rely on immigration

Over the coming years, the evolution of employment will depend both on the labor demand from firms and the supply of labor from workers. The extreme labor-market tightness experienced in 2022, when firms were posting 2 job openings per unemployed, has gradually diminished. By April 2024, labor markets appear to have normalized, but at full employment, with firms posting 1.2 job openings for each unemployed, roughly the same ratio as in the leadup to the pandemic, and the unemployment rate is near 4.0%. Assuming that employment remains at its potential (with firms continuing to absorb workers joining the labor market and the unemployment rate remaining near 4.0%), much of the evolution of employment will depend on the course of immigration.

## President Biden's executive action on immigration

On June 4, 2024, President Biden issued an executive action (EA) aimed at curbing record migrant arrivals at the southern US border.<sup>17</sup> The new order bans the entry of migrants who cross the border illegally, with the exception of some special cases. The order makes it substantially more dificult for humanitarian immigrants to apply for asylum or other types of parole. The ban is enforced once the seven-day average for daily US border patrol apprehensions at the southern border hits 2,500. If that figure drops to 1,500 for seven days in a row and stays there for two weeks, the ban is lifted.<sup>18</sup> The average number of apprehensions in April 2024 was well above the 2,500 limit cited in the order; in fact, it was nearly double the limit at about 4,300, suggesting that the order would immediately go into efect, and it will likely not be suspended in the coming months. For reference, the last time apprehensions at the southern border were below the 1,500 count was during the Covid-19 pandemic era.

However, the order also makes provisions for some exceptions. For example, the entry ban does not apply to immigrants who have scheduled appointments through the CBP One App with border oficers at oficial ports of entry ahead of their arrival to the southern border (there is a daily limit of 1,450 appointments), immigrants attempting to enter the US through non-southern ports of entry (mostly people who arrive by plane under humanitarian parole programs), unaccompanied children, and victims of human traficking. The entry ban also does not apply to legal immigrants, such as green-card holders or temporary resident visa holders.

## Three scenarios

We consider three scenarios regarding the future path of immigration:

1. A binding EA scenario, in which the Biden administration's recent executive action successfully restricts humanitarian immigration flows through the southern border as intended;

2. A business as usual scenario, where Biden's executive action has no meaningful influence, whether due to judicial intervention or other measures to circumvent restrictions; and

3. A severe scenario, in which inflows are restricted to rates that had prevailed in 2017-18, which overlaps with the initial stages of the Trump administration.

Figure 10 shows our projections for private NFP gains under these three scenarios. Since we focus on the NFP measure of employment (which assumes work authorizations), our immigration-related employment projections depend on the evolution of humanitarian immigrants, green-card holders and temporary resident visa holders. (Again, we abstract here from the unlawful entries and people overstaying.) In all three scenarios, we assume that greencard holders and temporary resident visa holders continue to enter the labor force at the same rate as in the prior year. Hence, the diferences in the three scenarios reflect diferent projections for humanitarian immigration.

FIGURE 10. Private nonfarm payroll gains under three diferent scenarios
![](images/8ec5357ace9ebd2d97db5e4da99d098f238b9fcf7395bf45c2fa9093a4817b40.jpg)
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Binding EA scenario

In the binding scenario, monthly increases in labor force coming from humanitarian immigration drop from around 113k in April 2024 to about 63k in June and thereafter.<sup>19</sup> If we include also green-card holders and temporary resident visa holders, this implies that the monthly flow of immigrants adding to private NFP will diminish from around 194k in May 2024 to just below 100k/m in 2025 (indicated by the blue line in Figure 10). Also adding roughly 44k US-born workers per month implies that private NFP gains should settle around 143k/m at the end of 2025.

Our projections of NFP gains continue to remain elevated through the end of 2024, despite the executive action assumed to remain in place. Indeed, all three scenarios result in similar projections through the end of 2024. This is because we account for a six-month delay between individuals entering the country as humanitarian immigrants and them receiving a work authorization. Consequently, humanitarian immigrants entering the country prior to the action should join payroll employment in the second half of 2024. If the lag for granting work authorizations is extended beyond this six-month period, that should also delay our projected decline in payroll gains.

## Business as usual scenario

In the business as usual scenario, indicated by the green line in Figure 10, monthly increases in labor force due to humanitarian immigration are assumed to remain steady at the average registered over the prior year, causing private NFP gains to reach around 164k/m by the end of 2025. Adding again roughly 44k US-born workers per month implies that private NFP gains should settle around 208k/m at the end of 2025 in that scenario, or 65k/m more than in the binding EA scenario.

## Severe scenario

In the severe scenario, indicated by the red line in Figure 10, monthly increases in labor force due to humanitarian immigration are assumed to drop from 113k/m to 3k/m, causing the monthly flow of immigrants joining private NFP to decline to 39k by the end of 2025, or 60k less per month than in the baseline. Adding again US-born workers implies that private NFP gains should settle around 83k/m at the end of 2025 in that scenario.

Even though this severe scenario is assumed to restore the inflow of immigrants of the 2017-2018 period, Figure 11 makes clear that overall private nonfarm payroll gains are projected to return well below levels registered in the pre-pandemic period. This is because the average monthly payroll gain of US-born workers has fallen sharply, from 145k in the 2015-2019 period, to 44k at present.

FIGURE 11. Private nonfarm payroll gains risk to fall below their pre-pandemic pace
![](images/5a17b6c947d16e1a8fe472df2b606ad15676bc033ecdb4a8c98a75bb07b76d79.jpg)
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Growth contributions of immigration

By afecting employment, immigration naturally also has an impact on overall economic activity and its potential growth. For simplicity, we characterize potential output, Y, in the nonfarm business (NFB) sector as the product of hourly productivity, Z, and potential employment (in hours), H, in that sector, and assume that the efects of immigration flows work mainly through the latter channel.

Given estimates of private nonfarm payroll employment, we apply estimates of weekly hours worked (workweek) to obtain total hours worked in the NFB sector. We also decompose these hours worked into hours worked by immigrants, $H _ { j } ,$ and hours worked by US-born, $H _ { U S } ,$ using our decomposition of payrolls discussed above.<sup>20</sup>

Next, using estimates of labor productivity in the NFB sector, or output per hour worked, from the BLS, we can decompose NFB output as: $Y = Z ^ { \star } H = Z ^ { \star } ( H _ { U S } + H _ { i } ) .$ . This implies that NFB output growth, $g _ { Y } ,$ can be expressed as the sum of productivity growth, ${ \mathit { g } } _ { Z } ,$ plus the growth contribution of hours worked by the US-born, $g _ { H u s } { ^ { \star } } ( 1  – s _ { i } )$ , and the growth contribution of hours worked by immigrants, $g _ { H i } { ^ { * } s } _ { i } ,$ where $S _ { j } = H _ { j } / H$ is the share of hours worked by immigrants: $g _ { Y } = g _ { Z }$ ${ } ^ { + } g _ { H U S } { } ^ { \star } ( { \cal { 1 } } { - } s _ { i } ) + g _ { H i } { } ^ { \star } s _ { i } .$

Figure 13 applies this decomposition to determine the contribution of US-born workers (light blue area) and immigrants (dark blue area) to NFB output growth. Unsurprisingly, since immigrants were contributing to only a small fraction of payroll gains until 2021 (Figure 11), they also had a moderate efect on NFB output growth. Indeed, much of the output growth in that period is accounted for by fluctuations in hours worked by US-born workers and productivity growth (grey area). Since 2022, with immigrants contributing a growing share of employment, they have also contributed a growing share of output growth. Indeed, in Q1 2024, much of the 3.1% y/y growth in output is attributed to productivity growth over the prior four quarters (2.9pp), and 0.9pp is due to hours worked by immigrants, while hours worked by USborn workers are exerting a drag of 0.6pp. Hours worked are held back in this period by a declining workweek.

FIGURE 13. Immigration started to have a noticeable efect on growth in 2023
![](images/1ec8799b2005061999727b2d38ec5023460db792c83e594a67a70d389b709637.jpg)
Source: BLS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Output growth in the three scenarios

Returning to our three scenarios, we can project the efect on potential NFB output growth. However, this requires a few additional assumptions. Specifically, we assume that productivity growth slows gradually toward 1.1% y/y by end 2025, as the level of productivity gradually returns toward its 2013-2019 trend line. Next, we assume that average weekly hours in the NFB sector remain unchanged.<sup>21</sup> Lastly, since we are making projections for potential output rather than actual output, we assume that the unemployment rate remains unchanged at 4%.

Note that if the economy were to unexpectedly slow due to a weakening of demand, such as consumer demand, the unemployment rate would likely rise, and actual output would weaken too, leading to lower growth. However, this would not afect the potential output of the economy, which is dictated by supply factors, such as productivity and potential employment, which reflect factors such as demographics and immigration.

As shown in Figure 12 and Figure 14, potential NFB growth is projected to reach 2.6% Q4/Q4 in 2024 in all three scenarios. This corresponds to a slightly lower growth rate for potential real GDP, of 2.3% Q4/Q4, given that GDP includes the public sector. The diferent scenarios have only minimal impact on the 2024 growth projection, again due to the delay between the entry of humanitarian immigrants and their work authorization.

For 2025, the growth trajectories would difer depending on the scenario, reflecting diferences in immigrant employment. As for employment, potential growth remains the highest under the business as usual scenario, with potential NFB output growing 2.9% in 2025, corresponding to a potential real GDP growth of 2.6%. Under the binding EA projection instead, the limitations on immigration would exert a bigger drag, bringing potential NFB output growth to 2.3%, corresponding to potential GDP growth of 2.1%. Lastly, growth would be slowing the most under the severe scenario, with potential NFB output growth dropping to 1.8%, corresponding to growth rates for potential real GDP of 1.7% Q4/Q4.

FIGURE 14. Immigration should have a significant efect on potential NFB output growth in 2024 and early 2025

## A primer on humanitarian immigration

Given that the increase in US immigration over the last year was mostly driven by the influx of 2.2mn humanitarian immigrants, it is worth focusing on this category in greater depth.

## Between 70% and 90% enter through the Southwest border

Entry to the US for humanitarian immigrants is evaluated on a case-by-case basis and usually depends on the individual's nationality and the way they enter the US. Our data show that between 70% and 90% of humanitarian immigrants enter the US through the Southwest border (Figure 15). The remainder typically arrive by plane<sup>22</sup> under country-specific or humanitarian parole programs (eg, for nationals of Cuba, Haiti, Nicaragua, Venezuela and Ukraine). Although the institutional details difer from program to program, generally speaking, immigrants who enter the US through these parole programs are allowed to apply for, and are very often granted, work permits.

## Pathways to the US

Those humanitarian immigrants who enter through the Southwest border, do it through two main ways. The first comprises people who illegally cross into the US between oficial points of entry and wait to be apprehended by US Border Patrol agents. Once apprehended, most immigrants apply for asylum, and those who pass screening and security checks,<sup>23</sup> are released into the US with a notice to appear before an immigration judge at some point (possibly a couple of years) in the future.<sup>24</sup> In the past, approximately 10% of all individuals apprehended by US Border Patrol agents at the Southwest border were ultimately released into the US, but that number has been rising, and in 2023 it was around 60% (Figure 16). Importantly, however, while their asylum claims are pending, humanitarian immigrants are protected from deportation and can qualify for a work permit.<sup>25</sup> We estimate that in 2023, close to 50% of all humanitarian entries followed this pathway.

In an attempt to make processing safer and more orderly, in January 2023, the CBP launched a new feature on its free online tool (the so-called CBP One application), which allows humanitarian immigrants to schedule appointments at Southwest border oficial ports of entry. If they pass the security checks at these appointments, immigrants are admitted to the US under the same conditions and work permit benefits as those who were apprehended at the Southwest border without an appointment. The daily number of appointments is limited to 1,450 and we estimate that in 2023, between 15% and 20% of the total number of humanitarian immigrants used this pathway.

## The majority of immigrants come from countries in Central and South America

The majority of humanitarian immigrants come from Colombia, Cuba, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Haiti, Honduras, Mexico, Peru and Venezuela (Figure 17). We also find that the composition of humanitarian immigrants changes over time and is typically triggered by political instability, economic crises or natural disasters in their homelands. For example, widespread political repression and escalating economic dificulties forced many Cubans to leave their country, and in 2022 Cubans represented the most numerous demographic among the humanitarian immigrants. Haitian migration escalated in the aftermath of the assassination of President Jovenel Moïse in July 2021, which led to a political crisis and exceptional economic hardship. This is reflected in our data, which show that Haitian immigration to the US has picked up in 2022 and 2023.

## 50% travel as part of a family unit

In Figure 18, we show humanitarian immigrants split by three groups: unaccompanied minors, single adults, and family unit individuals.

Approximately 50% of the individuals in this category are families. Taken together, the data in Figure 17 and Figure 18 underscore our assumption that humanitarian immigrants have a much lower incentive to leave the US.

## Green-card holders

## Gross immigration

Green-card holders are also known as Lawful Permanent Residents (LPR). We collect two data sets on LRP – the first comes from the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) (Legal Immigration and Adjustment of Status Report), which measures admissions of people obtaining LPR status and is available at a quarterly frequency; the second comes from the Social Security Administration (SSA) (The OASDI Trustees Report), which measures flows based on people entering the Social Security Area who are granted LPR status, and is available at an annual frequency. We combine data from these two sources, then we isolate the number of people who obtain LPR status and represent new arrivals to the country (as opposed to people who simply change status, eg, from temporary resident visa status to LRP status).

To transform these two LRP series to monthly frequency, we use the fact that I-94 non-tourist admissions are available on a monthly frequency and make the assumption that monthly LPR flows follow the same distribution within a quarter/year as the monthly I-94 non-tourist admissions in a given quarter/year. Then, we compute the share of monthly admissions within a given quarter/year, and use these shares to distribute the quarterly/annual LPR data across

months. Finally, we compute the gross green-card holders immigration time series as the simple average of the two monthly (ie, imputed) LPR series.

## Net immigration

We collect yearly data from the SSA (The OASDI Trustees Report) on the number of immigrants with LPR status and US citizens who leave the country (we include US citizens, because this group also contributes to the flow of people with legal status who leave the country). We transform the annual emigration data to monthly frequency data using the same process we followed for gross LPR immigration data.

## Labor force contribution

We use gross immigration data from the DHS (Yearbook of Immigration Statistics) by class of admission and by age group to separate working-age adults from children and senior adults. Then, we apply foreign-born civilian labor participation rates from the Bureau of Labor Statistics on this subset of working-age adults to estimate the share of people in the entire green cards granted abroad category who are potentially working at every point in time. Lastly, we apply these shares on the net immigration flow.

## Temporary resident visa holders

## Gross immigration

We use monthly data from two diferent sources to estimate gross immigration flows: nonimmigrant visa issuance, published by the Department of State (Monthly Non-immigrant Visa Issuance Statistics), and I-94 admissions data from the DHS (Yearbook of Immigration Statistics). Our final gross temporary resident visa holders immigration series is the simple average of these two monthly time series after applying the following adjustments.

From the non-immigrant visa issuance data, we remove visas types that only allow for short term visits to the US: tourists, crew members and people in transit (which correspond to B1, B2, B1/B2, BBBCC, BBBCV, C1, C2, C3, C1/D and D visa classes). We apply an X-13 filter to the monthly series to remove the seasonal efect of students applying for F1 visas in the summer, and then lag the resulting series by one month to account for the time gap between visa issuance and arrival to the US.

The I-94 admissions database measures the monthly number of entries to the US, by broad visa group. The I-94 form is a document issued by the US CBP to visa holders each time the individual enters the US through an oficial port of entry, and therefore it serves as a record of arrival. We remove I-94 admissions that correspond to tourist and business travelers(B1, B2 and BCC visa classes), and I-94 admissions under the Visa Waiver Program<sup>26</sup> (GB, GMB, GT, GMT, WB, and WT admissions). As the next step, we adjust the number of resident temporary-visa entries downwards by 65%<sup>27</sup> to obtain the number of unique temporary resident visa individuals. The idea is that because an I94-form is issued each time an individual enters the US, the I-94 admissions potentially overstate the unique number of temporary resident visa holders. Finally, we seasonally adjust the series by applying an X-13 filter.

## Net immigration

We estimate the monthly temporary resident visa holders emigration flow based on I-94 expected departures data from the DHS (Entry/Exit Overstay Report). The DHS provides the number of people in each visa category that are expected to leave the country using the I-94 admission forms, which specify the admission expiration date. As before, we only collect annual expected departures data for the visa categories that allow a temporary residency in the US and not a short-term visit. Then, we adjust the number of expected departures downwards to get the number of unique individuals who are expected to leave the US in a given year. We make this adjustment because the number of expected departures is built on I-94 entries data (not on unique individuals); also, we need to control for the fact that expected departures exclude I-94 data recorded at land ports of entry, and include people changing status to LPR. Finally, we compute actual departures by subtracting the number of people overstaying from the expected departures for each visa category.

## Labor force contribution

We take advantage of the visa issuance data by class to isolate work-visa holders, and a subset of spouses and students who are potentially working,<sup>28</sup> from children and other visa holders who are ineligible to work. In other words, we identify a subset of temporary resident visa holders who, given their visa status, must work. Then, we compute the shares of these mustwork visas in the total temporary resident visa issuance. Finally, we apply these shares on the net immigration flow series.

## Humanitarian immigrants

## Gross immigration

We start by collecting monthly data on apprehensions<sup>29</sup> ending in release<sup>30</sup> at the Southwest border from US CBP (Immigration Enforcement and Legal Processes). Book-outs data are only available for Southwest border detentions. However, this border accounts for most humanitarian immigrants entering the US (70% of total in 2023). We estimate monthly flows of humanitarian immigrants coming through other ports of entry (Northern border, inland airports, and entries by sea) by using nationwide data on detentions, applying to them the same release rates observed at the Southwest border. For the case of releases at non-Southwest border oficial ports of entry, we take advantage of data availability and compute these releases as the average between our estimates (using detention numbers and release rates) and available releases data from TRAC Immigration.

## Net immigration

To compute emigration rates, we assume a short-term emigration rate of 1.6%, which we apply on annual humanitarian gross immigration flows. Our humanitarian short-term emigration rate is half the size of the short-term emigration rate used by the literature,<sup>31</sup> as we believe that humanitarian immigrants have lower incentives to voluntarily depart the US and return to their countries of origin. For the same reason, we do not compute a long-term emigration flow for humanitarian immigrants.

## Labor force contribution

We use CBP data on releases by family status to identify the share of adults in this immigration category. Then, we apply these shares and the foreign-born civilian labor participation rates on net immigration flows.

## Unlawful entries and overstays

We present the computation methodology of these two immigration categories together as they are closely related to each other.

## Gross immigration

Unlawful entries are individuals who evade apprehension by CBP oficers upon entering into the US. They could be either detected but not apprehended (the so-called "gotaways") or not detected. We collect annual fiscal-year data on gotaways from the DHS (Border Security Metrics Report), and the Congressional Budget Ofice (The Demographic Outlook: 2024 to 2054). We distribute fiscal-year annual flows across months by assuming that annual flows are distributed across the fiscal year in the same proportions total apprehensions at the Southwest border are. We adjust the monthly gotaways upwards by 15%<sup>32</sup> to account for undetected unlawful entries.

For overstays, we compile annual fiscal-year data from the DHS (Entry Exit Overstay Report). We focus on in-country overstays; these are individuals who entered the US legally in a temporary status, but for whom no departure was recorded before the expiration of their status. Following the CBO (The Demographic Outlook: 2024 to 2054), we adjust these number downwards by 47% to account for people who may have overstayed for only a short time. Finally, we distribute the annual flows across months in the same proportions as monthly I-94 admissions flows are distributed within each fiscal year (see green-card holders for more details).

## Net immigration

We start by gathering monthly deportations data from the DHS (Immigration Enforcement and Legal Processes). In particular, we consider removals and returns where Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE ) was the initial arresting agency, as opposed to cases where CBP was the initial arresting agency. Thus, our methodology isolates instances where the individuals actually resided in the US at the time of their removal (which is diferent from instances where individuals were apprehended and turned back by border control oficers as they attempted to enter the US). As a second step, we derive long-term voluntary emigration flows. We apply a 2.5% long-term rate on the total annual unauthorized population stock from the DHS (Estimates of the Unauthorized immigrant Population Residing in the United States). We assume voluntary emigration flows to be equally distributed within a year. Then, we split the proportion of deportations and long-term emigration that corresponds to unlawful entries and to overstays,<sup>33</sup> respectively, using the same proportions of their monthly gross immigration flows. We also compute a short-term emigration flow for these two categories separately. In particular, we apply a 3.2% short-term emigration rate on each of their monthly gross immigration flows. Our long-term/short-term emigration rate approach is based on previous literature.<sup>34</sup> We set the long-term emigration rate at a lower level than the short-term rate to reflect the fact that the probability of emigrating decreases the longer people live in the US. This can be attributed to immigrants' lower incentives to leave the US once they have assimilated into the labor force, started a family, etc.

Finally, we compute emigration flows as follows. For the unlawful entries we add: 1) the proportion of deportations that corresponds to unlawful entries; 2) the proportion of long-term emigration associated with unlawful entries; and 3) its respective short-term emigration flow. We follow an analogous process for computing the emigration flow associated with overstays.

## Labor force contribution

We use annual demographics data from the DHS (Estimates of the Unauthorized Immigrant Population Residing in the United States) to identify the share of working-age adults in the unauthorized population. Similarly as before, we apply these shares and the foreign-born civilian labor participation rates on the net immigration flows.

Global Trade

# Back to the bad old days?

China's manufacturing overcapacity is colliding with eforts by the West to de-risk supply chains and protect strategic industries. The impact of recent tarifs is still limited, but risk of an escalation is rising. Some ‘connector’ countries may benefit from tarifs on China, but the global economy in aggregate would lose.

• Turning away from free trade back towards industrial policy and protectionism: The world is returning towards greater government intervention through trade restrictions and industrial policies. Recent policy moves by the US and Europe vis-à-vis China will likely prompt some tit-for-tat response, even though the trade confrontation should remain limited, including when compared with the 2018 'trade war' episode between US and China. However, risks are skewed to further fragmentation of global trade and investment, especially in case of a new Trump administration, which has plans for much higher tarifs.

• Lose, win, lose scenario: The US and China stand to lose (in economic welfare) from such a trade war. Some 'connector' countries (Southeast Asia, Mexico, Canada) could see relative gains from an (already ongoing) diversion of trade and FDI – at least temporarily, while US policies would likely aim at closing any loopholes. Eventually, reduced eficiencies and higher costs would imply welfare losses for economies globally.

## Long build-up of tensions

## Globalisation in retreat

By some measures, globalization has been slowing, if not in retreat, for some time. World trade in goods peaked some 15 years ago, around the time of the global financial crisis, following a period of rapid expansion that began in the early 1990s. While the crisis likely played a role in halting the steady increase of the goods trade as a proportion of global GDP, there was also an inherent leveling of after decades of outsourcing and optimization of global supply chains. An ever-greater division of labour and finer separation of production processes, with its related sharp increase in the trade of intermediate goods, had simply plateaued.

In parallel, voices against some of the negative consequences of globalization, such as disappearing manufacturing jobs in advanced economies (chiefly going to China) and the associated pressure on middle-income workers, had also grown. This was particularly true in the US, where some key political swing states in the Rust Belt were afected by the large transfer of traditional manufacturing jobs ofshore.

Moreover, oficials in the West have grown increasingly mistrustful of China in particular. President Xi has increased the central government's control over the economy and the 'Made in China 2025' plan issued in 2015 aimed explicitly at making China self-reliant and eventually dominant in key technologies. At the same time, the West was becoming increasingly concerned about what it sees as China's unfair trade practices, such as alleged disregard for intellectual property rights, state subsidies to domestic producers and barriers for foreign companies to enter China's domestic market.<sup>35</sup>

FIGURE 1. Global trade has plateaued but its share in global GDP has not meaningfully declined.
![](images/7bec88848dcd678f62bbf6c6532a7bf6e861ff52886496cef06c33b1d0bb4951.jpg)
Note: Slowing globalisation is sometimes referred to as ‘Slowbalisation’. The term was coined by Dutch writer Adjiedj Bakas. For more details, see Douglas A. Irwin (2020). Source: Jordà-Schularick-Taylor Macrohistory Database. Data 1960 onwards from IMF and World Bank, Barclays Research

## US-China trade war in 2018

Many of these concerns culminated in President Trump's trade war in 2018, when his administration imposed tarifs on thousands of products, valued at approximately \$350bn at the time, with rates between 10% and 50%<sup>36</sup> . China was the primary target of these measures as Trump aimed to address unfair trade practices and intellectual-property theft; the average US tarif on imports from China rose from 3.1% in January 2018 to 21.0% in December 2019 (Figure 2).

That said, the tarifs were not limited to China; for instance, Trump ended previous exemptions on steel and aluminium tarifs for the EU, Canada, and Mexico. However, the tarif increase on countries other than China was generally limited. In response, China and other nations responded by raising their own tarifs – China's average tarif on US imports rose from 8.0% to 21.8%.

Ultimately, the trade war reached a ceasefire in January 2020 with the signing of the Phase One agreement and, later, the end of the Trump presidency. Some of the tarifs on other trading partners, such as EU steel and aluminum, were also suspended at a later stage. This reduced tarifs the US and China apply to their trade slightly, but they have remained at around 20% ever since.

That said, even as US-China tarifs have durably reset to a higher level, with costs for the two countries and benefits for some others, the 2018 trade war did not fundamentally redraw global trade relations.

## The quest for resilience and de-risking

The COVID pandemic intensified concerns about China, due not only to how the government handled the outbreak (eg, transparency about the virus’s origin, warning other countries<sup>37</sup> ) but also because it highlighted the high dependence of global manufacturing supply chains on China<sup>38</sup> . China's lockdowns suddenly made it evident how often the global production of certain goods relied on crucial inputs produced in just one specific Chinese city or region.

As a consequence, it became an overarching demand by Western politicians for corporate supply chains not only to aim for the highest cost eficiency, but also to become more resilient. This implied de-risking from China by implementing 'China +1' supply-chain strategies and 'near-' or 'friend-shoring' production. In addition, the US has responded to China's increasingly assertive stance in the Pacific region by restricting US high-tech exports (eg, high-performance chips) to China and limiting Chinese involvement in sensitive sectors at home (eg, 5G networks)<sup>39</sup> .

## Current dynamics towards escalation

## China's drive for manufacturing dominance

## Expansion of "new productive forces"

In a further confrontational  twist, the quest by the US and EU to reduce supply-chain dependence on China and regain strategic autonomy in key industries has been met by a diametrically opposed strategy on the part of China. The leadership in Beijing turned its focus to gaining dominance in high-tech products and related manufacturing sectors, especially those required for the energy transition. For instance, last September, President Xi vowed a'"new leap forward' by focusing on the so-called 'new productive forces,'<sup>40</sup>  such as EVs, quantum technology, and new materials. Importantly, this is not only a domestic goal, but also an export goal. More recently, Chinese oficials have named EVs, batteries, and solar cells the 'three new export drivers,'<sup>41</sup> and Chinese manufacturers have enhanced their competitiveness in these and other high-tech products by a decade of industrial upgrading and technological innovation.

FIGURE 4. China dominates in many products needed for the green energy transition
![](images/7e284b27561bae2eea993049c457118cd070462ad0005e21df3e940b736064ea.jpg)
Note: Capacity is for physical facility location, not manufacturer headquarters. Lithium refers to lithium hydroxide and carbonate Source: Jennifer A Dlouhy, April 16 2024, "China Extends Clean-Tech Dominance Over US Despite Biden’s IRA", BloombergNEF

## Weak domestic demand fuels overcapacity

This focus on new manufacturing is a continuation of China's earlier strategy to shift the economy from property investment and dependence on cheap manufacturing exports towards domestic consumption, services, and higher-value-added manufacturing. However, with the efort to boost domestic consumption and services demand stymied by the ongoing severe slump in the country's property sector, the government's strategy towards boosting supply in prioritized manufacturing sectors has been further bolstered. After all, stimulating investment in large-scale manufacturing has been a time-tested success of China's state-driven economic regime.

Growing manufacturing supply combined with weak domestic demand has inevitably led to overcapacity.  As expected, even though China has a long history of overcapacity, this has intensified over the past few years. China's capacity utilization in Q1 24 was the lowest since the 2015-16 economic downturn (excluding a one quarter drop in the early stage of the pandemic).

In addition, this is widespread, with falling utilization rates in most sectors, but especially auto manufacturing, property-related sectors, consumer communication devices and machinery equipment. For more tangible examples, one can look to the EV industry, where production capacity in 2025 is expected to reach over 36mn vehicles, a 20mn surplus over expected sales in that year<sup>42</sup> . Similarly, China's annual production capacity for finished solar modules at the end of 2023 was more than double the global module installations<sup>43</sup> . A similar observation can be made in the battery industry<sup>44</sup> .

## The West's return to industrial policy and protectionism Economic theory vs reality of political economy

From a theoretical perspective, tarifs against Chinese manufacturing imports are not necessarily the optimal response for the US, Europe and other importing countries. Accepting the inflow of Chinese EVs and solar panels would significantly improve the afordability of such goods for domestic consumers, thereby helping to accelerate their ambitious energy transitions. Importing inexpensive basic EVs and solar panels from China would, in principle, free up domestic resources to be deployed in other, possibly higher-end tech areas.

Even without state subsidies, which to some extent also exist elsewhere, China simply has significant cost advantages in manufacturing (eg, labour costs, scale). The fact that Chinese EVs are typically sold at much higher prices in Europe than in their home market suggests that there is no outright 'dumping' involved. Tellingly, the European Commission imposed tarifs on Chinese EVs not only without European car manufacturers ever asking for such protection, but even against their explicit plea not to impose such tarifs.

However, the structural changes and likely distributional consequences are not palatable from a political economy perspective. Job losses in domestic industries would likely make more negative headlines than the large, but widely distributed, aggregate gains from much cheaper EVs. Furthermore, there is also the fear of potentially losing competency in sectors of strategic importance in coming decades and thus becoming dependent on China. Hence, what may have been acceptable 20 years ago – using China as an eficient workshop – is no longer possible in a world where China is seen as a strategic competitor and security challenge.

FIGURE 9. EVs are sold at much higher prices in the US and Europe than in China
![](images/817cb2f4d4a76f25a901234cf4812f5b52f1dea5b4ca7df523739c256782e206.jpg)
Source: 'The average EV retail price in the biggest markets', EV Markets Reports, Oct 2022

## The US' new industrial-policy paradigm

Initially triggered as a response to the pandemic but continued as a new industrial-policy paradigm to transform the US economy and tackle climate change, the last three years have seen a series of large-scale government spending programs: the bipartisan infrastructure bill (Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act), the CHIPS and Science Act, and IRA (Inflation Reduction Act). These policies are centered on subsidising investment in the US with a broad aim of transitioning to new technologies and clean energy.

Given the combined size of these programs, they clearly qualify as a paradigm shift: it is estimated that annual real federal spending on climate and clean energy over the next decade will be at least 3.5-times its 2009-17 level and 15-times its level in the 1990s and early 2000s.<sup>45</sup> These subsidy programs are flanked by the imposition of tarifs to protect the strategic investments they aim to promote (eg, EVs, solar panels; more below). Moreover, under the Biden administration's "small yard, high fence"<sup>46</sup>  national-security policy approach, heavy restrictions are imposed on specific technologies and business activities.

FIGURE 10. Industrial policy is on the rise globally...
![](images/26d9b3f65e1076e19145a49051cea50d93a30ecf8abb1801236c2bb322e12e92.jpg)
Source: Juhász, Lane & Rodrick (2023), "The New Economics of Industrial Policy ", NBER working Paper 31538

FIGURE 11. ...and now features prominently in public discourse
![](images/ac08d6a28484afc9ba35fedb9f3ef78d6dce443fc00a73f22e542900811953b3.jpg)
Source: Evenett, Jakubik, Martín, Ruta (2024), "The Return of Industrial Policy in Data", IMF Working Paper

FIGURE 12. The US started to subsidise domestic production in similar areas where China aims for dominance
![](images/a80d8fbc8bce32734c46ca56b1858fba769530ec2f724fc7306dc30be75ac757.jpg)
Source: 'Congress’s Climate Triple Whammy: Innovation, Investment, and Industrial Policy', RMI, Aug 22, 2022

## The EU's attempt at a middle ground

The EU's NextGenerationEU (NGEU) economic recovery package passed in 2020 had similar objectives as the US programs, of boosting demand during the pandemic while also transforming the European economy by assisting the green transition. However, while in principle the NGEU is comparable in size to the US IRA, it works mainly through national governments, comes with many eligibility criteria and generally is not as readily accessible for industries as the IRA. Indeed, the IRA is widely seen as a threat to Europe, as it is accessible to European companies only if they produce in the US and thus ofers an incentive for European firms to redirect investments to the US.

Importantly, the EU has generally been more reluctant to be as strict and confrontational as the US in its trade and security policies vis-à-vis China, given its much greater dependence on China as an export market. The EU has also imposed restrictions on Chinese activities, including takeovers of high-tech companies by state-linked Chinese firms and, most recently, it increased its tarifs on Chinese EV imports (albeit they are much lower than those imposed by the US). In principle, the EU remains much more committed to maintaining a global trade regime under multilateral institutions such as the WTO.

## Limited efects so far, but approaching risks

## New tarifs on China by the US and Europe

In the US, the Biden administration announced in May that it would retain Trump's tarifs and significantly increase them in certain strategic sectors. For example, the tarif on Chinese EVs would quadruple to 100%, and other products, including solar panels, semiconductors, lithium batteries, steel, and aluminium, would also see tarif increases. In doing this, the administration cited concerns about unfair trade practices and China's overcapacity. It also emphasized that the tarifs targeted strategic sectors where the US is making its own investments.<sup>47</sup>

Similarly, after an almost year-long investigation, the EU last week imposed tarifs on EV imports from China. They range from 17% to 38%, and, since they come on top of the existing 10% import tarif, they raise the overall rate to 27% to 48%. Although these may seem moderate compared with the 100% tarif adopted earlier by the US, it makes the EV market one of the most protected sectors in the EU. In addition to EVs, the EU has now also opened investigations into the state subsidies behind China's wind turbines and China's public procurement procedures for medical devices.

Even though the recent tarif measures reflect important changes in policy direction, when put in context, they have been measured: for example, they remain much smaller than the tarif increases seen in the US-China trade war of 2018. The new tarifs imposed on clean technology by President Biden only afect about \$18bn of US imports – less than 0.5% of total US imports. In addition, only a minuscule share of EVs sold in the US are currently imported from China. On the other side, Chinese EV producers' exposure to the US is also limited, as only 16% of Chinesemade EVs are exported, and the US is not a major destination<sup>48</sup> . In comparison, the tarifs imposed by the US on China during the 2018 trade war afected around \$350bn of US imports<sup>49</sup> , about 12% of total US imports.

Similarly, in Europe, imports of all vehicles from China represent only c.0.5% of total imports (though this has risen from c.0.0% in 2019), implying that aggregate cost to the economy could be small. At the same time, our equity research colleagues calculate that Chinese car manufacturers currently make enough profit on their EV sales in Europe to fully absorb even a 30% import tarif and remain competitive compared with European EV makers. In addition, even at that level, the European market remains more profitable for BYD than the Chinese market.

## China's retaliation

Of course, China may retaliate, and the authorities have vowed "resolute measures" after Biden's new tarifs, though little detail<sup>50</sup> about the scale, form, or timing of the response is available at this stage. For Europe, China had threatened the EU's aviation and agricultural exports (including, for example, items such as French brandy) in retaliation to the EV tarifs<sup>51</sup>  as well as a 10pp increase in the tarif on imported cars with large engines to 25%<sup>52</sup> .

In all, the measures implemented so far by all sides are far from the 2018 trade war. Further titfor-tat escalation seems likely, but the actions taken by the US and the EU have been measured, and in the past, China's reaction was proportional (Figure 2). Most likely, the recent actions are part of the long-running and gradual slowing of trade between these parties. This does not make them irrelevant, particularly for specific sectors of the economy in some countries, but, in the aggregate economy, the slower globalization trend matters more than these particular actions.
