## Risks skewed towards further escalation

## A Trump administration could bring additional tarif increases

However, the risks are clearly skewed toward a more drastic escalation, especially in the event of former President Trump's reelection. If President Trump were to implement tarifs as proposed (60% on China and a universal 10% tarif on all imports), that would clearly be a substantial increase in tarifs and a very sharp break from the post-WWII trends. However, we believe this scenario is rather unlikely; our Washington Research analyst notes that the former President sees himself as a dealmaker and, as such, tarifs are his negotiating leverage. Thus, Trump could threaten tarifs against China or even preemptively raise them to some extent to bring China to the negotiating table, but the modest revenue from tarifs is unlikely to be an end in itself. In short, Trump's re-election could bring another major trade war and a significant escalation of tarifs, but the exact magnitude is dificult to pinpoint.

But in addition to higher tarifs, there are also three other areas where our Washington Research analyst believes Trump's trade policy would diverge from Biden's. First, the previous Trump administration preferred bilateral rather than multilateral trade policy. For example, Trump's approach was to impose tarifs unilaterally on China, rather than working through the WTO or with partners. Second, Trump’s negotiating style is to be unpredictable, which means the ultimate policy implemented may well be less (or more) harsh than the one threatened; though, at the same time, the higher uncertainty carries costs of its own. Third, former President Trump's focus is diferent than Biden's, aiming for lower trade deficits and trade reciprocity, compared with Biden's push to advance his climate agenda and worker-centric trade policies.

FIGURE 14. Trump's new tarif proposal would be a sharp departure from the post-WWII trading regime
![](images/67b07dcf4c81ee40f51f36b105a4a9cc953228780f87da05b1f5726c7272b0c4.jpg)
Note: \*Assuming Trump's new tarif proposal of 60% on all Chinese imports and 10% on all imports from the RoW, we estimate an import-weighted average tarif to be c.17% Source: USITC, US Department of Commerce, Bureau of Census, Historical statistics of  the United States 1789-1945, U.S. International Trade Commission, Barclays Research

## China could scale up retaliation in response

As in 2018, China would almost certainly respond tit-for-tat to a meaningful increase in tarifs. In addition, we note some other non-tarif retaliation options which could be employed in a new trade war:

• Export restrictions: Limiting exports to the US of some critical resources such as rare earths. This was a tool used in 2010 when China retaliated against Japan over Diaoyu island issues. Although US and allies such as Australia and Canada have been trying to increase their production capacity for many rare earth minerals, China remains the dominant producer of many critical raw materials and rare earth elements. For instance, China controls >70% of both light and heavy rare earth elements, and 89% of the magnesium market<sup>53</sup> .

• Restrictions on sales of US goods and services: China could potentially restrict sales of certain US products and services in China, citing national security concerns or for regulatory or legal violations. The restrictions could apply to US smartphones, servers, autos, pharmaceuticals, or distribution of Hollywood movies and series. We think such actions would be constrained by considerations of whether or not their imposition would incur significant domestic economic and/or employment costs.

• Financial tools such as devaluing CNY and selling of US Treasuries: in the event of further escalation of the tarif war, China could allow measured weakness in CNY. That said, we think the Chinese authorities would be unlikely to tolerate a very sharp and sudden depreciation. Moreover, China could reduce its UST holdings, although it would also hurt itself because selling would reduce the value of the remaining portfolio. Since the start of the trade war in 2018, China has cut its UST holdings to below USD800bn in January 2024 from close to USD1.2trn in early 2018.

• Restricting the exports of lower-end chips (used in autos) and other electronic components may also be an option, which would add pressure to already elevated inflation in the US. However, we note Taiwan ranks No.1, with a dominant \~40% global market share in this segment (logic chips), versus China’s \~20% share.

• Other non-tarif restrictions targeting US companies trading with or operating in China: the countermeasures in our view, could include intensifying shipping inspections and prolonging customs checks, applying greater scrutiny or delaying approvals of licenses and permissions for doing business in China, and tightening regulatory discretion over investments by US companies.

• Imposing restrictions on travel to the US: the US services trade surplus (mainly in the travel, intellectual property categories) with China was USD40bn in 2018.

• Delaying or canceling oficial and civil exchanges and visits between the two countries, likely through tightening approvals and visa requirements.

## Global losses and some relative gains

## US and China to both lose from trade war

Regardless of the extent to which trade disputes escalate, the textbook mechanism for how it afects the involved countries – disrupting the gains from comparative advantage – and the main conclusion – that aggregate well-being in both countries is worse of – are clear. However, to quantify potential consequences, one might assume the reasonable worst-case scenario in which Trump's new tarifs are imposed as proposed (60% on China, 10% across the board).

For China, the primary cost would come through reduced exports, and using a simple extrapolation of the 2018 experience, we estimate the proposed tarifs could depress China's exports by about 5%, equivalent to about 1% of GDP. For the US, consumers would carry the bulk of the burden in the form of higher taxes (as tarifs get passed through to final consumer prices) and eficiency gains, and one think-tank estimates<sup>54</sup> that these would amount to c.1.8% of GDP.

However, the actual implications of any future trade war, whatever form it takes, are certain to be more complex than such simple guesstimates can capture. For one, they do not account for the diverse efects tarifs are likely to have across countries and sectors. As an example, the European EV tarifs that are supposed to protect domestic producers may end up causing more harm than good to those firms if China retaliates.

In particular, while China's profit margin in selling EVs in Europe is large enough to absorb the tarifs, our equity strategists calculate that European car manufacturers export to China at a much lower margin, making them less competitive in that market. As a result, some of them have actually lobbied against the tarifs<sup>55</sup> . Moreover, the efects difer even across car sectors in diferent European countries – German car manufacturers are more reliant on exports to China, and thus more likely to be hurt by its retaliation, while French producers could benefit from the tarifs.

## Some 'connector' countries could benefit from trade diversion

In addition, while it is clear that a trade war is a lose-lose for those directly involved, at least in the short term, it could also be a relative win for other countries. These countries could benefit if part of the trade flowing from, say, China to the US, in some way now passes through them to avoid the tarifs. Indeed, data support this hypothesis, showing that between 2019 and today, China has tended to increase exports to the same countries where the US has increased its imports (Figure 15). These include Mexico and Southeast Asian countries such as Vietnam, Indonesia, and Thailand.

That said, these gains could be short-lived as countries imposing tarifs on China close the loopholes. For instance, recent news reports suggest a number of Chinese companies have halted output at solar plants set up in Malaysia, Thailand, and Vietnam after the Biden administration tightened its tarifs on solar equipment by ending the exemption available to those countries<sup>56</sup> . Similarly, there are concerns that Chinese-owned companies are benefiting from US subsidies under the IRA, leading to the introduction of the NO GOTION Act in US Congress, which would bar companies associated with "countries of concern", including China, from receiving IRA tax credits<sup>57</sup> .

FIGURE 15. Amid rising US-China trade tensions, Mexico and Vietnam stand out as 'connector' countries: increased their exports to the US, while in parallel receiving more imports from China
US China trade diversion
![](images/6b0498d3ad6f2a2f9ad468dd3ea4c502b38972f8307c3f595e8390e18e88de0e.jpg)
Note: Bubble size = share of US imports coming from the country, 2023
Source: IMF, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Beyond tarif circumvention, though, bystander countries benefit in relative terms if they are a substitute for China's products if the US imposes tarifs on China. Furthermore, they may even expand their exports above and beyond this, to other countries. This can stem from factors such as eficiency gains from greater economies of scale due to exporting more to the US or increased investment and technological improvements in the country.

There are numerous examples of such trade diversion. The Financial Times, for example, recently declared Malaysia "the surprise winner from US-China chip wars," noting increased FD

Share effects in the change of exports between 2017 and 2023 into its tech manufacturing sector as a result of tensions between the two economic giants<sup>58</sup> . Apple is shifting more of its iPhone production to India, reducing China's dominance in its supply chains<sup>59</sup> . Samsung closed down its last smartphone factory in China in 2019, opting for India and Vietnam instead,<sup>60</sup> and Vietnam has also been attracting more of the Apple supply chain<sup>61</sup> .

To take this a step further and make it more systematic, we update the methodology we used to analyze the efects of the 2018 trade war.<sup>62</sup> As shown in Figure 16, the biggest winners of the trade diversion from 2017 to 2023 in absolute (USD) terms have been a number of Asian countries (Vietnam, Taiwan, Thailand, Malaysia), Mexico, and Canada, which have all increased their market share of US imports. Some of them, including Indonesia, Malaysia, and Vietnam, have also increased their share of Chinese imports, alongside Russia and Brazil. In contrast, Japan has lost market share in both the US and China, though it is unclear whether this is related to the trade war. Relative to the sizes of their own economies, some smaller countries, including Slovakia, Mongolia, Costa Rica, Oman, and Slovenia, have also benefited from changing trade patterns.

FX Strategy

# Currency wars, China-style

China's stealth currency debasement has supported trade competitiveness, but provoked a backlash from the G3 while fuelling capital outflows. JPY and KRW weakness is adding more pressure on the CNY. As the USD rallies broadly, the CNY and much of Asia will need to follow suit, with potential tarifs posing additional risks.

China's stealth currency debasement...: Under the surface, deflationary Chinese policies in a period of high global inflation have cheapened the CNY's real efective exchange rate and have bolstered (alongside broader industrial policies) China's trade competitiveness.

...is triggering unintended consequences: Politically, the backlash from the totality of Chinese policies is leading to further protectionist measures and tarif risks from both the US and Europe<sup>63</sup> . Beyond rising oficial tensions, China's deflationary policies are fuelling capital flight and undermining the balance of payments position.

Pressing the issue: Large depreciation in currencies with similar export markets, including Korea but chiefly Japan, is translating into trade efects and is pressing the issue. We show that the JPY can continue to passively depreciate vs the USD and CNY even if US rates do not rise further but stay persistently wide to Japanese rates.

In a broad dollar rally, China may need to follow. We show this would not be by necessity, but would be a choice. Stealth depreciation of the CNY is starting to reverse and China's exports  – the economy's main growth engine – are showing early signs of moderation. Tarif risk post the US elections in November amplifies the risks. The path of the CNY will determine the outlook for Asian currencies. We show that cyclical Asian currencies (eg, KRW, THB, SGD, MYR) will likely follow the CNY, while carry Asian FX (INR, IDR) will show a smaller beta.

## China's stealth currency debasement

One of the least appreciated drivers of the low volatility FX environment of the past year or so has been the CNY, which has weakened by much less than would be expected against the USD, despite diverging interest rate diferentials. Specifically, in past cycles, every 100bp spread widening between US and China 2y rates translated roughly into 4.6% depreciation in the CNY against the USD (Figure 1).

Using the same beta, the divergence in rates observed since early 2022 (560bp) would have been expected to result in 26% CNY depreciation vs the USD, yet USDCNY only experienced a \~16% trough-to-peak rally in the current cycle. We think this is largely attributable to aggressive CNY fixings (Figure 2) by the PBoC and ongoing intervention by Chinese state banks.

## Taking a look under the hood tells a diferent story

A superficial look at the Chinese exchange rate gives ground to the notion that, over the years and especially since COVID, the CNY has been appreciating on a trade-weighted basis, not depreciating.

This nominal efective exchange rate (NEER) appreciation is a result of a conscious efort by the Chinese authorities to maintain a semblance of stability against the USD (Figure 2). There are several reasons for their approach: 1) to limit capital outflows and to entice foreign capital inflows; 2) a weaker CNY may provoke criticism from the US administration and run the risk of being labelled a currency manipulator; 3) a tight grip on the currency helps to promote CNY internationalisation; and 4) a stable currency is an important domestic political anchor.

All told, in an environment of dollar strength, relative CNY stability has led to a de facto reduction of volatility (Figure 3) and has capped the dollar too. We discussed this in detail in the March edition of the FX & EM Macro Strategy Quarterly Outlook.

However, a closer inspection reveals a diferent story. China has been consistently running much lower rates of inflation, even deflation, amid an environment of rapidly accelerating global prices. In relative terms, Chinese prices have declined. And this has had deep exchangerate implications.

Indeed, under the radar, China has been debasing its currency. A CPI-based Real Efective Exchange Rate (REER) shows marked depreciation, using PPI an even deeper decline. There has also been a sharp divergence between the CNY NEER and REER (Figure 4). The weakness in the REER is a result of policies started during the COVID years – an adherence to boosting domestic demand and a focus on promoting manufacturing to climb the value ladder. As such, we believe the market is not taking stock of the full picture.

FIGURE 3. CNY has been a major driver of low G10 and EM FX volatility
![](images/d4d9e3da653d3e0fc42129b786cbab3ae07f4f828f07dcd90e78ac06e9a51162.jpg)
Implied vol Index weights based on BIS FX turnover. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research
FIGURE 4. CNY NEER appreciating, but REER tells a diferent story, one of FX weakness

![](images/ea361e2dfce11219961e374bec69b493bd9b4b7ed1e915fd9739dffe5ec1631d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Stealth depreciation provokes increasing G3 backlash

China's stealth CNY depreciation has provided it with 1) a competitive advantage, 2) an incentive to limit inflationary policy stimulus, and 3) less risk of being labelled a currency manipulator by the US Treasury. However, combined with broader measures to boost various industries, this situation has not gone unnoticed by other nations.

The weaker CNY REER has provided a bufer for Chinese exporters, providing an outlet for various products amid overcapacity onshore, and resulting in oversupply ofshore, most notable of which has been EVs lately. Resistance from the US, European Union and others to growing Chinese manufacturing oversupply and investment has increased. This has led to an overall moderation of China - G3 trade. But some of that is likely trade diversion (see 'Global Economics: Overcapacity meets de-risking: back to  trade war?' within this publication, for more on this).

Due to G3 sanctions, tarifs and investment restrictions, China's trade with Asia has been increasing (Figure 5), ofsetting somewhat slower trade with the G3 (Figure 6). Some of this trade has found its way to the US via third countries, blunting the impact of tarifs. This has also meant that Asian supply chains and, in turn, currencies have become increasingly linked to China and the CNY (Figure 25).

FIGURE 5. Chinese trade with Asia increased amid trade diversion...
![](images/87cecd1fd0bd9e1b029e70a89634bd8a4d667b9871153946c7147478834e147a.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 6. ...while trade with the G3 falling amid growing resistance to CNY debasement
![](images/68148ef8868c4fa494f8f70733295e155c00b5c15e4cc8dee377bb689b45a3f5.jpg)

## Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

While we can debate the overall eficacy of China's policies, especially in the long run, it is hard to argue that the policy intention is clear and that it has supported Chinese trade activity in the short run relative to the counterfactual.

Given the role of Asia as both a re-exporting hub and a competitor, China's FX policies have had a deep impact regionally, and the influence of CNY moves on local currencies is becoming increasingly important, as we detail later.

Recently, however, things have started to change. This trend of real CNY depreciation has stopped or has even started to reverse at the margin. This is due to two factors: a) inflation diferentials are narrowing; and b) the nominal exchange rate is appreciating in trade-weighted terms.

## Negative unintended consequences

## Political backlash exacerbates trade competition

Of course, it is not just relative prices that have contributed to China’s industrial and export resilience. Another pillar in China's industrial model lies in the new centrality of the industrial priorities for Beijing, which first showed in its "Made in China 2025" policy, included in President Xi's new development model in 2017. Public investment in industrial firms has bounced back over the past decade, through government guidance funds (GGFs<sup>64</sup> ), and SOEs<sup>65</sup> .

While not every cent of GGF investment can be considered a subsidy, the overall size of more than CNY3trn as of 2023 still suggests a large amount of cash piled into Chinese industry (Figure 7). The momentum on the "Made in China 2025" programme lost steam during 2017-19, likely due to distractions during the US-China trade war, but has regained strength since the pandemic. We present an alternative calculation of Chinese industrial support by diferent sources in Figure 8.

As argued earlier, China's policy eforts to deepen its trade competitiveness, whether linked to industrial policy or relative prices (real efective exchange rate), have triggered a backlash and policy responses (especially from the US) and more recently from Europe, specifically on Chinese EVs.

These responses have included the US IRA and CHIPS Act, which we expect to drive longer-term dollar strength versus G10 and Asian FX. We highlighted in US-China trade: Chipping away how controls on US semiconductor exports to China could have substantial efects on China's output and currency, if escalated further.

Since then, controls have escalated. As we noted in US-China: Rhetoric on outbound investment, this is likely to reduce the fundamental support for the CNY and overall USD supply to China, resulting in a much slower buildup of FX reserves or even a drawdown in the months and years ahead.

FIGURE 7. Industrial subsidies in China are rising post COVID
![](images/2c11bb7133dc70275861b39ba9e65140efda43404f09c227a1268c57fece072d.jpg)
Source: China NBS, CVSource, Zero2IPO, Barclays Research

FIGURE 8. The channels of Chinese industrial support by sources (% of GDP)
![](images/6d237e439dd3b9cc87fb64a459c1c80eb1b0f9ff0d8104ea6d00732dd0bef336.jpg)
Note: All series are simple averages of annual data as a share of GDP over the period covered. OECD data has been converted to percentage of GDP by applying the 2005-19 average ratio of total annual domestic industrial enterprise revenue (from the Chinese National Bureau of Statistics) to GDP. Source: NBS, CSRC, OECD, CSIS, Barclays Research

## Deflationary policies trigger capital flight

There have, however, been unintended consequences beyond and above politics and international trade frictions. Deflationary pressures in China have weighed on domestic demand, and ultimately domestic asset market returns.

In turn, weak economic performance – ex trade – has fuelled expectations of CNY weakness. Consequently, foreign investor inflows have tanked and domestic capital outflows have increased. China's broad basic balance position has worsened more than it did in 2015 due to deflationary policies.

Underlying support for the CNY is also fading due to a worsening picture in terms of portfolio and direct investment flows (Figure 9), as well as a shrinking current account surplus. The capital and financial account has remained weak, with a positive balance registered in only three of the 12 months to April 2024.

Direct investment has been negative for 23 straight months, highlighting not only how longerterm foreign investors have shifted away from China, but also how Chinese companies are increasingly investing in other countries, as far a field as Mexico<sup>66</sup> , to avoid US sanctions.

Securities investment flows have been mixed, with inflows in around half of the past 12 months, while the current account has been positive over recent quarters, helped by firming exports and import compression. However, as noted above, China is increasingly reliant on exports to Asian countries as the G3 balk at oversupply. Also, as Chinese overseas tourism picks up, the services balance of the current account could deteriorate quickly, as it did pre-COVID.

Consequently, China's broad basic balance position shrank last year to only USD47bn, less than half the previous year's total, and less than a tenth of the surplus two years ago (Figure 10). The trend is clear and we do not expect any improvement in the months ahead.

Ultimately, this has reduced the underlying fundamental support for the CNY, rendering the currency more vulnerable to capital flight, necessitating more oficial support in the months ahead, in our view.

FIGURE 9. Direct investment flows to China have collapsed...
![](images/dab63a2a53fc5945c355a3ad8b4f99783db1350cd9d9ba4f7501cc4d7df15931.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 10. ...contributing to a sharp deterioration in the broad basic balance
![](images/317cf7fdb2fd19ccfefbd30368861386064419901070e11fdec2249ebc318023.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Risk of tarifs looms large again

Moreover, the risk of tarif escalation is once again rising in the near term. Europe's EVA tarif is expected to come in at an average of 21% for EV makers that co-operated with the EU investigation and as high as 38.1% for those that did not<sup>67</sup> . At the same time, either a potential second Trump term or Biden administration may result in higher tarifs. We discussed at length the implications and potential for ensuing USD strength in our last Global Outlook.

Yet tarifs may simply lead China deeper down the same set of policies in a form of currency/ trade war (Figure 11). First, while tarifs will raise the prices of Chinese exports, they could also add even more downward pressure on the Chinese currency. Indeed, we estimated a direct boost to the dollar of up to 3% from tarifs, with confidence efects likely amplifying the impact. In particular, we estimated that 60% tarifs on Chinese imports, with retaliation, would imply an average 3.1% decline in the CNY NEER.

FIGURE 11. USDCNY moved sharply higher amid tarif announcements and implementation in 2018 while the USD broadly strengthened
![](images/d01e2350c9da381872c8b5a66ffb6268c64445754cca745c607c06fb32048cc2.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Second, through trade diversion, China may be able to mitigate some of the impact of tarifs. Indeed, there has been a high degree of China trade diversion via South East Asia and other countries (Figure 5). We calculate that diverting trade via third countries adds another 2% to Chinese exports to the US and that the actual drop in Chinese exports to the US may be far smaller than US trade data suggest (Figure 12).

Third, ongoing domestic deflation also lessens the impact of tarifs. Chinese producer prices have been falling in year-on-year terms for 20 straight months. As shown in Figure 13, US import prices have largely corresponded with Chinese PPI trends, suggesting limited price pressures for US importers.

FIGURE 12. Misreporting suggests previous tarif impact was more limited than previously thought
![](images/300b478ce02472a7490ed98e4dd558a11ff599735bb061dcd1981bac99f83daa.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 13. Limited price pressures for US importers
![](images/8f58b03c25caab52376894151f01a7f759e9c7512990c5e22da0ffafcebb734e.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Persistent rate diferentials and the JPY are now pressing the issue

None of the above would be actionable without a catalyst pressing the issue. Instead, all of the above can be seen as part of a slow-burning, muddle-through situation, where nominal exchange rate moves may take time to manifest.

This is a misconception. Beneath the apparent calm, nominal depreciation pressures on the CNY are building up. The efects of "higher US rates for longer" on the broad dollar and also, very importantly, regional competitors such as the JPY (or the KRW), are likely to act as catalysts.

To be sure, neither the persistent higher US rates regime, nor the strong dollar impulse are exogenous to the macro concepts described herein. In part, higher rates and dollar strength are linked to the investment or broader spending implications linked to US fiscal policies – aimed at addressing trade tensions with China – including the IRA and the CHIPS Act. And equally, the gap between inflationary US policies and deflationary Chinese policies is aggravating the issue.

Within this context, a CNY quasi-"fixed" to the USD is appreciating passively despite being backed by diferent policy intentions and measures. As we will discuss later on, there is still space for the CNY to appreciate to simply catch up with the delta in relative prices. Still, the move is in the wrong direction.

And while the move appears to be moderate in trade weighted terms, against some key competitors with high export similarity, namely the JPY, the move is enormous (Figure 15).

FIGURE 14. Export similarity indices in North Asia
![](images/076104726a0e5667f123d0184a6e07b589d5410a8b3136c33f6245631d5a1b42.jpg)

## High degree of export similarities means CNY appreciation is painful

Europe, China, Korea and Japan have a high degree of overlap in export products. To measure the degree of export similarity between these markets, we constructed an Export Similarity Index (ESI) based on their export HS codes (for methodology, see this note).

Our ESIs (Figure 14) suggest still-elevated Europe-Japan export similarities, while that for Japan-Korea has been on a declining trend, which coincided with the rise in the Korea-China ES

and China's Made in China 2025 agenda, as well as our discussion earlier on Chinese government subsidies to industry to boost export resilience.

Notably, the Europe-China ESI has been on a rising trend in the meantime, which points to likely further policy responses from Europe (eg, EV tarifs discussed). The high degree of overlap in export products also make CNY outperformance over the JPY and KRW conspicuous (Figure 15).

Specifically, with the case of the yen, we can see how a large bilateral exchange rate move is benefiting Japan's products over China's. In fact, China's share in Japan's overall imports has more than reversed its post-COVID gains amid the sharp rise in CNYJPY (Figure 16).

FIGURE 16. China share in Japan's imports vs CNYJPY
![](images/f5f81b8febdb8a69ce9a265bc84d79ef781f5221a121c780bf75f6d30a597992.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 17. Carry creates a persistent drift on JPY
![](images/54ab325a9ac5db29d0c55b4d6fa69d68101151279eb1c5963c5afc64f75f05c4.jpg)
Note: For details of the carry factor, please see FX Views: Scandies: a carry problem. The index shows cumulative monthly changes since Jan 2006. Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Extended JPY depreciation adds more pressure

Interestingly, JPY depreciation looks set to extend as long as US rates stay high. Large and persistent rate diferentials have the capacity to create a negative drift for the JPY linked to carry dynamics (Figure 17).

During past periods when carry trade was the key market theme (ie, carry trade ofers positive return) and US-Japan policy rate diferentials exceeded 400bp, USDJPY tended to exhibit an upward drift even if rates diferentials started to tighten (Figure 18). In the pre-GFC carry period (mid-2006 to mid-2007), USDJPY retained its gradual uptrend by rising 13% even as 2y US-Japan yield diferentials tightened by 50bp over the same period. USDJPY only started to fall once the markets started to actively price in sharp Fed cuts ahead.

FIGURE 18. USDJPY under carry trades
![](images/588958d1b1fd9ceb9d6efeb67b40555d708c9d44d92edeec4e15105c1a3a379e.jpg)
Note: "Carry period w/ ID>400bp" is where (1) G10 carry trade position (long 3 highest carry and short 3 lowest carry, excluding JPY) has 4w-rolling FX spot return exceeding 1sd and (2) US-Japan policy rate diferential exceeds 400bp. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 19. FX neutral spread with US for G10 and EM Asia
![](images/ce90738362d2e27ed687f26c20e495d15b2ce22dbe442e6f35303b067010a9a1.jpg)
Note: For details of the methodology of our FX neutral spread with US, please see the appendix in FXQ: Stronger for longer but not stronger forever.
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

The recent JPY experience has been a case in point. JPY depreciation has exceeded domesticoverseas rate diferentials since the start of the year and advanced even with tightening diferentials more recently, while JPY carry trade positions have accumulated steadily. One reason the JPY has depreciated more than implied by domestic-overseas rate diferentials may be that absolute diferentials are too large.

We estimate the US-Japan rate diferential needed to contain this excess JPY weakness is around 4.0-4.5% and expect that level to be reached only around next spring. First, our estimate of the JPY’s “FX-neutral rate diferential” is at 4.25% (Figure 19). Second, USDJPY dip-buying tends to accelerate at rate diferentials of 4.0-4.5% or larger (Figure 20). Third, in CFTC noncommercial JPY positions, there is a tendency for net JPY shorts to rise at rate diferentials of 450bp or higher (Figure 21). We look for further BoJ rate hikes and the start of Fed rate cuts in H2 24, but with US-Japan rates diferentials still not reaching 4.0-4.5% level this year, JPY depreciation pressures are likely to persist.

While the JPY is most exposed to this excess weakness from wide rates diferentials, other lowyield FX are not immune either. In fact, some other G10 (eg, AUD, CHF) and many EM Asia currencies also exhibit large gaps between the FX-neutral carry vs spot, putting them under similar pressures as the JPY (Figure 19).

FIGURE 20. US-Japan policy rate diferentials and USDJPY performance

![](images/78011a73ced03d082992b9ebfedcd821e1613ea2669b587487b25e9fc4a591f3.jpg)
Note: "Dip-recovery hit ratio" is the probability of USDJPY intraday declines of a certain size retracing completely by the EST5pm on the same day. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 21. US-Japan policy rate diferential and JPY positions of noncommercial accounts
![](images/6b850ad2a17c813c06d35e2ba88ee71b17c8b33aff6bd0193a15efe87012d6aa.jpg)

## In a broad dollar rally, China may need to follow

If the dollar appreciates significantly in the months ahead, the Chinese authorities might no longer chose to opt for stability. It is true that they have ample policy space and discretion to keep the CNY at their preferred level. It is also true that, politically, it may be to China's benefit to keep the currency inconspicuous heading into the US election (although, as we wrote in our previous Global Outlook, a possible win for Trump could imply the imposition of broad tarifs, which could lead to a substantial depreciation in CNY equilibrium).

Yet, in the event of significant passive appreciation of the CNY (due to invariance to the dollar), the direction of travel for relative prices would move even further against their policy intention. This is especially true as, at the moment, exports are increasingly one of the most important drivers of economic growth in China. The net exports contribution to GDP growth rose to 1.4pp of the 5.3% registered in Q1, from a drag of -0.6pp to 5.2% growth in 2023, while the domestic demand contribution from consumption and investment declined 1.3pp.

## Optimism over China's exports may fade quickly

Recent data complicate the picture for export-led growth further – and raise the stakes for the CNY. Indeed, 1) recent weakening in China's new export orders, 2) some softening in US consumer demand, and 3) the increasing backlash against China exporting its overcapacity in certain products (Figure 22) suggest that optimism on China's export outlook may recede, even as China broadens its exports markets towards EMs, especially in Southeast Asia (Figure 23) and the Belt & Road Initiative (BRI) countries.

What is more, tarif risk may exacerbate the recent loss of momentum. If China is hit with 60% tarifs, an appreciating trend in the REER would become increasingly unpalatable. Ultimately given the importance of exports in driving economic growth, such a loss of competitiveness would imply increasing resistance to CNY REER appreciation. Higher relative prices may also spur REER appreciation, though we think relative FX moves will need to do much of the heavy lifting in terms of competitiveness.

## Asian currencies will follow

In the event of a broad dollar rally, with CNY participation, Asian currencies are likely to depreciate in sync. This is due to economic linkages and policy spillover, more than a lack of currency defence tools. More cyclical currencies such as the KRW, THB, SGD and MYR feature higher betas vs higher yielding currencies such as the IDR and INR.

Specifically, it is true that Asian authorities have become more innovative and efective in defending their currencies, while limiting reserves losses at the same time, as we noted in Asia and the USD - Defence, the best form of ofence (Figure 24). For much of the region, ratios such as FX reserves/imports and FX reserves/short-term external debt have improved over recent years, as have broad basic balance positions.

Nonetheless, despite better external resilience metrics and reduced likelihood of sharp drawdowns in FX reserves, Asian currencies will likely face more pressure in the months ahead, especially if the CNY depreciates as we expect. Much will depend on the magnitude of CNY depreciation.

We find that Asian FX is more sensitive to gyrations in USDCNY than moves in the DXY (Figure 25). The most sensitive currencies are the KRW, THB and MYR, with the INR, PHP and IDR at the other end of the scale. It is no coincidence that the most sensitive currencies are low yielders and as such, we think they will be most exposed as the CNY comes under pressure.

Based on these betas, we model a scenario where the CNY drops to 7.5 against the USD (around a 3.4% fall) and estimate that the KRW, THB and MYR would fall by around 4.7%, 4.3% and 3.8%, respectively. As such, while the Fed, and broader USD will be important, the path of the CNY will be the biggest driver of the outlook for Asian currencies.

FIGURE 24. Asian FX reserves mostly flat or higher despite a stronger USD
![](images/a8a8ad1fa6cc20e2de55f64cb393a15e6c30afa4ba2d74b76b1e73f4931eec4e.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 25. Asian FX more sensitive to gyrations in USDCNY than DXY

## Appendix - A review of China's FX war chest

We often get asked whether China is likely to lose control of its currency. We would argue that it is much more likely that any depreciation of the CNY would be the result of deliberate action, rather than forced.

In the event of substantial FX depreciation pressure, we would expect the Chinese authorities to step up their defence of the CNY via aggressive fixings, liquidity measures, cuts in FX reserve requirements, moral suasion to domestic market participants, and direct FX intervention, among other measures. As we have written previously, China's authorities have many options in their tool kit.

We look at monthly changes in the PBoC's FX assets, monthly data on net FX settlements and valuation-adjusted FX reserves to estimate the level of FX intervention (Figure 26). We find that much of China's FX interventions continue to take place via state banks. Monthly changes in the PBoC's reported FX assets show that it made around USD28.2bn in USD purchases between January and April 2024, supporting the view that direct intervention by China's central bank is very limited.

Similarly, in the first five months of this year, China's reported FX reserves declined by around USD6bn. However, the fall in reserves was attributed to the strength of the USD rather than actual intervention by the PBoC. We estimate that FX reserves would have been around USD34.8bn higher over this period had it not been for USD strength.

However, Chinese banks' net FX settlement balances show that they made USD sales to market participants of around USD62.9bn during January-April 2024. This appears to suggest that much of the intervention to sell USD and support the CNY has been carried out by state banks. However, this is hardly a large amount when put into perspective of the amount of USD holdings of China's state banks, which we estimate at around USD1.5trn (Figure 27).

When combined with total oficial FX reserves of more than USD3trn and FX assets held by other state entities such as CIC, EXIM and CDB, China has a formidable war chest with which to defend the CNY. However, when examining China's FX reserves alone, even at USD3trn, they are relatively low on some metrics such as reserves/broad money ratio of reserves/IMF ARA metric,

Barclays trading desks may have either a long and / or short position in such securities, other financial instruments and / or derivatives, which may pose a conflict with the interests of investing customers. Where permitted and subject to appropriate information barrier restrictions, Barclays fixed income research analysts regularly interact with its trading desk personnel regarding current market conditions and prices. Barclays fixed income research analysts receive compensation based on various factors including, but not limited to, the quality of their work, the overall performance of the firm (including the profitability of the Investment Banking Department), the profitability and revenues of the Markets business and the potential interest of the firm's investing clients in research with respect to the asset class covered by the analyst. All levels, prices and spreads are historical and do not necessarily represent current market levels, prices or spreads, some or all of which may have changed since the publication of this document. Barclays Research Department produces various types of research including, but not limited to, fundamental analysis, equity-linked analysis, quantitative analysis, and trade ideas. Recommendations and trade ideas contained in one type of Barclays Research may difer from those contained in other types of Barclays Research, whether as a result of difering time horizons, methodologies, or otherwise.

Environmental, Social, and Governance (‘ESG’) Related Research: There is currently no globally accepted framework or definition (legal, regulatory or otherwise) of, nor market consensus as to what constitutes, an ‘ESG’, ‘green’, ‘sustainable’, ‘climate-friendly’ or an equivalent company, investment, strategy or consideration or what precise attributes are required to be eligible to be categorised by such terms. This means there are diferent ways to evaluate a company or an investment and so diferent values may be placed on certain ESG credentials as well as adverse ESG-related impacts of companies and ESG controversies. The evolving nature of ESG considerations, models and methodologies means it can be challenging to definitively and universally classify a company or investment under an ESG label and there may be areas where such companies and investments could improve or where adverse ESG-related impacts or ESG controversies exist. The evolving nature of sustainable finance related regulations and the development of jurisdiction-specific regulatory criteria also means that there is likely to be a degree of divergence as to the interpretation of such terms in the market. We expect industry guidance, market practice, and regulations in this field to continue to evolve. Any references to ‘sustainable’, ‘sustainability’, ‘green’, ‘social’, ‘ESG’, ‘ESG considerations’, ‘ESG factors’, ‘ESG issues’ or other similar or related terms in this document are as used in our public disclosures and not to any jurisdiction-specific regulatory definition or other interpretation of these terms unless specified otherwise.
